i do not think that anyone in the european parliament would accept that homosexuals should be discriminated against, attacked, intimidated or anything else because of their sexual orientation. that is not really the subject of today's debate. the debate is about whether it is still possible to criticize the spirit of political correctness that is gradually eroding freedom of expression. fortunately, the time has passed when homosexuality was taboo, but today it has become taboo to express concerns about some behaviour or tendencies of some homosexuals, even if it has nothing to do with discrimination or hatred or anything. the buttiglione case is still fresh in the memory. for example, those who oppose gay marriage are now quickly labeled homophobes and criminalized. commissioner frattini was just talking about diversity. but now, it's important that we also keep an eye out for diversity of opinion, because the 19th century quackery of sexual morality is being gradually replaced by the quackery of political correctness.

the rapporteur. - mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i should like to begin by thanking the shadow rapporteurs from the other groups for their good cooperation. we have not always been able to agree on all the points, but i think we have reached agreement on the substance and will thus contribute to the establishment of a european strategy on a subject which will only grow in importance in the coming years. the reason for this is, as you know, the famous open skies ruling of the court of justice in 2002, which established that the european community has exclusive powers in the field of international air services, and more specifically in relation to automated reservation systems, intra-community ticket prices and the allocation of slots or landing rights. the court of justice has also ruled that the commission's decision to impose a fine on a third party for infringement of the competition rules is not justified by the fact that the commission has not taken into account the principle of proportionality. there are two instruments for this: either the member states themselves are conducting bilateral negotiations with the third country to bring their bilateral agreement into line with the case law (58 agreements have been brought into line in this way), or negotiations are being conducted at community level under the so-called horizontal mandate, and 22 countries have already accepted a horizontal agreement with the community, which has led to the adaptation of 327 bilateral agreements. so in total 385 agreements, a long way to go before all 2,000 agreements have been amended. in my report i therefore advocate that all bilateral agreements be adapted as soon as possible to the open skies judgments in order to avoid legal uncertainty. i prefer community agreements, since it only takes one negotiation to replace all existing bilateral agreements with a particular country at once. the european community can have a much greater say in negotiations than, for example, a single member state, but this requires a common coherent vision. i would stress that we must conclude agreements with our main partners and the developing countries, such as china, the united states and russia, as soon as possible, but we must not forget our immediate neighbours either. this is important for economic and aviation policy reasons, such as safety and security, and that is why i have tabled another amendment on this for today's vote. when community agreements are concluded, it is essential that the european community takes account of three important elements. firstly, the agreements must be balanced and equal access to each other's markets is important. this means that aspects such as landing rights, cabotage rights, equal rights of establishment and ownership and also state aid must be addressed. i myself have tabled an amendment for today's plenary session to add the question of state aid to the list. this is not unimportant - just think of the increased security measures imposed on all airlines in the united states after 9/11, but which were funded by the us government for american airlines and not for other airlines and that means distortion of competition. secondly, my report stresses that the opening up of markets must always be preceded by regulatory convergence. and that the degree of liberalization must be linked to fair conditions of competition. the european union has, or is working on, legislation on a number of social matters, such as safety, security, the environment, state aid and competition. these high standards cannot be eroded by the arrival of market players who have to abide by less stringent rules. the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the draft directive on the protection of the environment in the event of a serious accident. otherwise we are dealing with distortions of competition and there is also the danger of flagging, which would lead european airlines to move to states with less clear rules. that is why i have tabled an amendment to include the convergence of regulations on security, state aid and competition in my report, so that legislative convergence will also take place in these areas. if an open skies agreement proves impossible, the commission is asked to develop fair and transparent mechanisms for the allocation of traffic rights among member states. a third point, finally. the commission should also regularly consult and keep informed all the parties involved in the aviation sector and the european parliament during the negotiations on the many new aviation agreements to be concluded between europe and the rest of the world in the coming years. these three important elements, mr president, are also reflected in mr zīle's report on russia and china. here too the principle of reciprocity must be applied. the high charges levied by russia for flying over siberia must be abolished, because the shortest route to fly to asia - which is becoming increasingly important - is the russian federation. so i would like to give my full support to mr zīle.
mr president, the catania report which we have just voted down, the report on citizenship of the european union, can be credited with being without doubt one of the most hallucinatory and stupid texts ever produced in this parliament, and that is saying something. it was a matter of granting universal suffrage for all elections to everyone who was more or less legally in our territory. there was no requirement for integration, no requirement for language skills, no nationality requirement, in short, all rights but no duties for foreigners. this was indeed madness, this was the work of a few blinded ideologues who did not want to acknowledge the reality of the totally failed multicultural model. moreover, the report fundamentally undermined the sovereignty of our member states. citizenship and all rights attaching to it, including the right to vote, should remain the exclusive competence of the member states. europe has nothing to do with that. we have rightly voted against this stupid report.
ladies and gentlemen, sugar is the most heavily subsidised agricultural product in europe today. this makes the price three times higher than the world price. it's an unfair and unacceptable situation. i therefore believe that a thoroughgoing reform was inevitable, is inevitable. the question is whether the commission's proposals go far enough. i think that the structural problem of the sugar sector, in particular a general overproduction, is being overlooked. already, far more sugar is being produced than is being consumed. the price reductions will increase this, which will only benefit the large producers and the distribution. finally, it is the agro-industry that will benefit from the cost savings, while the small farmers will receive less for their sugar beet yields. at the end of the journey, the consumer will still pay the same price for a kilo of sugar. the poorest countries in particular are in danger of being hit hard by this reform. the preferential treatment has led to the creation of local sugar plantations in several acp countries, often on a small scale and with low investment. without further european support, these countries will be unable to compete. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect, and i am sure that the commission will be able to support them. the other amendments tabled by mr kinnock deserve our support. failure to meet the commitments under the everything but arms programme will seriously damage our credibility with the mol. in addition, this reform invites discussion on the general abolition of all export subsidies, as promised again recently in hong kong. in summary and conclusion: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them.
mr president, madam commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, today's debate and the vote that is to follow will bring to a close more than a year of work in this house. i would also like to emphasize the constructive spirit of the rapporteur, mr jean-claude fruteau, whose reports i support. however, more than an end, this moment marks the beginning of a change that will take place in the sector. i would say to the commissioner that the drift towards the 'all-for-the-market' approach is devastating. we've helped to alleviate it a little bit. if this reform is rightly considered inevitable, we must be wary of its effects, since, after the council and parliament have agreed on the position, it is based on a drastic reduction in prices: 36% in four years. deregulation through prices is beneficial to large producers who can continue to expand without respect for the environment and exploit even more, outside europe, an underpaid labour force. this is also beneficial for the large users, who will buy sugar much cheaper on the world market without passing on this decrease to the selling price of the finished product. this is their definition of development, not to be confused with our concept of development! deregulation through prices, on the other hand, is harmful to small producers in our country and, even more so, in the acp countries and the ldcs, who will sell their sugar cheaper and may have to stop production, but without any real alternative to try to live off other production. this will not be beneficial for small consumers either, since it is very likely that the price of sugar will not fall for them. in addition, in europe, some countries will stop production and others will continue with difficulties, both for producers and for industries and their workers. i would like to make a special point about belgium: i would welcome the possibility of restructuring the chicory sector with a view to the production of insulin, which is a promising future. i tabled amendments to that effect and i thank you, ladies and gentlemen, for accepting them. i would like to see more attention paid to the part of the aid to farmers and the conditions to be met by industrialists applying for restructuring aid in the february council. i'll be very careful!
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the provisions of the aarhus convention are applied by all the community institutions. and, despite what some of my colleagues may say, there is absolutely no intention of going beyond what is necessary to enable the community to conclude the aarhus convention. i am very pleased that the committee on the environment has substantially strengthened the council's common position. i therefore fully support the report and hope that it will be adopted in its entirety without amendments. given the growing importance of community environmental policy, the challenge is therefore to ensure that the regulation complies with all the provisions of the aarhus convention, not least in order to maintain some credibility with the member states and at international level. in other words, i believe that the same obligations and rights should exist at eu level as at member state level. this is particularly true for the identification of possible grounds for refusal of environmental information. the main point of discussion - and we have heard this before - is the transposition of the third pillar of the treaty, namely access to justice. it is clear that the general interest of the environment cannot be defended solely by persons who can assert a direct or individual interest, as provided for in the treaty. the commission has therefore decided to restrict access to justice to environmental organisations which meet certain conditions. and to try to impose shortcuts or additional conditions on this is not only unacceptable but also contrary to the aarhus convention; i therefore sincerely hope that those of you who are in favour of an open and transparent environmental policy in the eu will adopt the report as it stands.

vice-president of the commission. mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to thank the commissioner for his reply. i have only two short things to say. the first is to quote elmar brok who said at the beginning of the debate: 'the citizens are the stakeholders'. it is the citizens of europe who are the main target group, those who have the most to gain or lose from the way we deal with the question of a new constitutional treaty for europe. the second is to say that democracy is not a spectator sport. it requires that we engage in debate and dialogue with citizens, engage our political leaders and our citizens in every way possible and help us play our different roles in that context. however, i have heard so many of these rather easy remarks about 'what is it about the negative that you do not understand' and so on, that i would like to add that it is actually the case that we know precisely through opinion polls and interviews why people have answered yes or why they have voted no. it is not something we have invented, we know that any referendum which also includes constitutional questions carries the risk of actually getting answers to questions which have not been asked. politicians in all our member countries know this. it is not surprising that 25 member states engaged in a debate on how to solve a constitutional challenge in a europe which has grown from 15 to 25 member states are actually wondering how to get out of a situation where two member states have said no when 14 have said yes. how do we deal with that situation? the president of the council. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. the conflict in chechnya continues to be a source of instability in the north caucasus region. the political, economic and social situation and the human rights situation in the caucasus republic remain extremely unsatisfactory and worrying. all the institutions of the european union are well aware of the importance of this issue in relations with the russian federation. as you know, on 28 november last year parliamentary elections were held in chechnya, the first legislative elections in eight years. unfortunately, the european union and the osce were not able to observe these elections for security reasons. the fact that, for the first time in eight years, parliamentary elections were held in chechnya - although it must be stressed that they were very flawed - was regarded as a positive step by the then council presidency. the european union has supported the training of local observers for these elections. the european commission awarded a grant of eur 60 000 to support objective and professional local election observation by various civil society organisations. immediately after the elections, the then eu presidency issued a statement stating that the conduct of these elections could not be considered to be flawed and that some observers had expressed concerns. it urged the russian authorities to investigate the reported irregularities and intimidation. the declaration concluded that further strengthening of democratic institutions as part of an inclusive political process is crucial for the long-term sustainable and peaceful development of chechnya and for peace and stability in the north caucasus as a whole. the european union has always expressed serious concern at the political process in chechnya and continues to urge the russian authorities to give greater openness and legitimacy to this political process. human rights and democracy are regular and ongoing issues in relations between the european union and the russian federation. the partnership and cooperation agreement, in its fundamental statements on the eu-russia relationship, provides for respect for common values such as democracy, human rights and the rule of law. chechnya is a key player in the biannual human rights consultations with russia, which were officially launched in march 2005 under the luxembourg eu presidency. in these consultations, the eu expresses its concern in particular about the disappearance of persons and the fact that human rights violations generally go unpunished. the last round of these very important consultations took place in brussels on 8 september 2005. they will be continued under the austrian presidency of the council this year. a sustained commitment by the european union and its members is needed here, as these consultations provide a framework in which such issues can be addressed in a very open and constructive way. the european union has taken and maintained a clear position on the issue of human rights since the outbreak of the second conflict in chechnya in september 1999. the union continues to advocate a political solution to the conflict and calls on the russian federation to cooperate closely with international human rights mechanisms, in particular with the united nations and its special rapporteur on torture and other cruel, inhuman or degrading treatment or punishment, the council of europe, in particular the european committee for the prevention of torture and inhuman or degrading treatment or punishment, and, of course, the osce. the osce would certainly welcome a renewed permanent presence in chechnya in the form of a field mission. the osce has already played a very important role in the efforts to find a solution during the first conflict in chechnya. a similar role would be of great importance this time too. at the eu-russia summit in november 2004, russia agreed to the european commission's €20 million aid programme, which is intended to support socioeconomic recovery in the north caucasus in addition to ongoing humanitarian aid. the programme is intended to contribute to the promotion of an open and democratic political process in chechnya. as regards civil society, the situation of non-governmental organisations, judicial reform, the situation of human rights activists and media freedom in russia are areas of particular concern and are regularly raised by the eu with russian representatives. the eu is firmly convinced that it is in russia's interest to have a strong and independent civil society. the draft law on non-governmental organisations was adopted by the state duma and the federation council at the end of december 2005. the eu has repeatedly expressed its concern about this draft, in particular in a letter of 22 november 2005 to duma speaker gryslow and at a troika meeting on 2 december 2005. i also expect that now that the bill has been signed, we will take a stand again. the draft law requires foreign ngos to provide the russian authorities with reports on their programmes, funding and organisation. russian ngos may be prohibited from working in restricted areas, which by its nature makes their activities in sensitive areas and areas very limited or impossible. the g8 presidency of russia since 1 january 2006 should provide an incentive for russia not only to improve its image on the international stage but also to contribute to a more active and sustained effort to resolve the conflict in chechnya and thus also to improve the situation in the south caucasus, where three other conflicts - though frozen - with south ossetia, abkhazia and nagorno-karabakh are awaiting urgent resolution. the eu has stated that it will closely monitor russia's conduct during the g8 presidency. the issue of civil society will continue to be raised at the semiannual human rights consultations with russia. mr president, we must be firmly resolved to continue the dialogue with the russian federation on chechnya and other human rights issues, despite all the difficulties and resistance, and to press for an improvement in the situation.
member of the commission. mr president, president-in-office, honourable members, i am most grateful for this opportunity to give the commission’s view on the future of chechnya after the elections. it is positive, as the presidency mentioned, that the elections took place without violence, even if one cannot ignore the deficiencies in the process. three parties received sufficient support to be represented in the new parliament, with the pro-kremlin united russia far outweighing the others. we hope that this constitutes a step towards greater political representation, greater stability and eventually peaceful democracy. neither the osce nor the council of europe sent a fully fledged observer mission, since the conditions on the ground did not permit it, but the latter did send an eight-member fact-finding mission. we therefore have no official reports, but we are aware of statements by members of the latter mission and by other organisations drawing attention, in particular, to deficiencies in the process, and we expect such allegations to be properly investigated. while we welcome the russian federal authorities’ commitment to allocate significant funds for reconstruction, a number of other actions need to be taken if these elections are to be a step towards more peace and democracy. although there appears to have been an improvement in the security situation in chechnya, unfortunately a culture of impunity remains. reported cases of disappearances and torture should be fully investigated and the perpetrators, including members of the law enforcement authorities, brought to justice. we note that russia has expressed its willingness to cooperate with un human rights mechanisms including, for instance, the high commissioner for human rights, mrs louise arbour. these assurances should be put into practice and there should be cooperation with the un special rapporteurs. it is also important that the local ombudsman in chechnya be seen to be carrying out his duties impartially, as well as effectively. as has already been mentioned, i would like to emphasise that the european commission is playing its part in the region. we have agreed a eur 20 million programme for socio-economic recovery in the north caucasus, because we believe that to be one of the root causes of that conflict. this is in addition to humanitarian assistance, for which we are already the biggest donor through echo. this gives a clear indication of our willingness to be actively involved in the process of strengthening democracy and stability in the region. our programme will help support the much-needed rehabilitation of the health and education sectors and assist in job-creation and income-generation activities. in my former capacity as austrian foreign minister i visited chechnya as chairwoman of the osce. i remember my visit very well. we saw health establishments at first hand and a lot of improvement is indeed necessary. it is also our intention to open an office in the north caucasus region, possibly in vladikavkaz, which would help us to monitor the implementation of our programme and keep us better informed on the in writing. the debate on the major european issues, as it has been in recent months, is largely emotional and less reflective. whether it's the constitution, the services directive, reach - to name a few - our challenge is to simplify language and communication so as not to leave the field to the populists. few institutions have so many structures and resources to support communication, but few institutions use a language whose complexity leaves room for intentional or unintentional misinterpretation. the debate on europe too often forgets two aspects: to mention together what has been achieved and to explain what is new in this context. there are essentially two major 'schools' for the future of europe. the constitution has shown, among other things, how those who want more europe and those who want less europe can bring europe to a standstill by voting together but never by thinking. the commission's proposal is to be submitted to the council for adoption. the duff report is going in the right direction, but action must follow.

the commission's vice-president mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the considerable development of road transport over the last twenty years has led the commission to present two texts. on the one hand, a proposal for a regulation made in 2001 to simplify, clarify and update a well-known regulation for drivers, no 3820/85, which defines driving times, breaks and rest periods for drivers. on the other hand, a proposal for a directive made in 2003 to replace a 1988 directive which ensures a minimum level of control of driving and rest times in road transport. we all worked to finalise this package on 6 december 2005, after a long and difficult negotiation that lasted more than two years. the conciliation committee finally reached an agreement. parliament has made a great contribution to the progress of this matter and i would like to congratulate the chairman of your delegation, mr vidal-quadras roca, to whom mr markov has paid tribute. i would also like to pay particular tribute to the work of mr markov, mr grosch and mr piecyk, and the members of the committee on transport and tourism, who have followed the issues very closely and have made real progress with regard to the text of the council's common position. a compromise was reached on two thirds of parliament's amendments to the regulation and on more than half of parliament's amendments to the directive. i shall take the example of the introduction of a community system for establishing a common and precise classification of offences according to their gravity. such harmonisation will help to clarify the obligations imposed by the regulation, both for industry and for controllers across europe. i am talking about the whole of europe, not just the european union. at the insistence of parliament, the council and the commission have committed themselves to ensuring that the aetr agreement can be ratified by the other european countries, including russia, within two years. it is true that we have not succeeded in including the control of working time in the scope of the new directive. but i can assure you that the commission will do everything possible to ensure that the working time directive is fully and completely implemented. on 19 december the commission sent 10 reasoned opinions to the member states which had not yet notified the full transposition of the directive. the conciliation process has also led to further progress. this legislative package will generate a number of advantages and improvements over the current situation. i'm sure you do. i will give you a few examples. clear and simple limits on weekly driving hours make the text easier to understand and, of course, make it easier to monitor compliance. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. finally, of course, the increase in controls, the extraterritoriality of sanctions, the accountability throughout the transport chain. i would like to stress once again, mr president, that this progress has been achieved in large part thanks to the interventions of parliament. the controls in the field will be more coordinated, more efficient. it is not just a question of increasing them, but also of making them more efficient. finally, a word on the digital tachograph. i want to say here this morning in parliament how desirable it is that the texts under consideration should be given a favourable approval because they will enable us to make the use of the digital tachograph effective. parliament agreed with the council on a deadline for its introduction. parliament and the council issued a joint statement on this subject. pending your agreement to the legislative package, i informed the transport ministers of all member states on 12 january 2006 that i for my part would agree to extend the grace period until the deadline. i made this gesture to avoid misunderstandings. but it is clear that, beyond the date set by the legislator, that is to say, by parliament, any new lorry which is not equipped with the digital tachograph will be subject to immediate sanctions by the national authorities, including its immobilization. mr. markov, you asked me about three things. i would like to point out that the commission has immediately started work to give you the answers you are looking for. i hope that you will agree with the outcome of this conciliation and that we can finally close this case more than two years after the commission's proposal. it would be a real pity to delay the progress contained in these texts, which can then, thanks to your vote, be applied in the european union.

in writing. at least one in three european women will have been the victim of physical or sexual violence at some point in their lives. this is the startling figure drawn from the excellent report by my liberal colleague, maria carlshamre. in the face of the escalation and the normalization of violence against women, there is only one response, dignified and firm: "zero tolerance", within and outside marriage. europe has taken its responsibility to combat this form of violence which affects women from all social classes. the daphne ii programme has allocated eur 50 million for 2004-08 to protect the most vulnerable among us. this is a symbolic amount, because we know that in order to be effective, prevention and victim support policies must be implemented at national level. how shocking it is that each year between five and ten million children are witness to such inhuman acts! it is therefore essential that the 25 national criminal laws now consider children, as well as their mothers, as victims. our society has underestimated the seriousness of violence against women for too long. we must put an end to this cowardice!
in writing. to deal with equality between men and women is first and foremost to be aware of the whole path traveled for more than a century and the first struggles for women's emancipation, but it is also to keep in mind all the gap that still needs to be filled in order for gender equality to be realized in everyday life. i therefore welcome the adoption this afternoon of mrs estrela's report, which points to the various forms of discrimination against women and therefore to the many challenges to be met. to name just two: an estimated 16% pay gap, and a stagnating employment rate for women aged 15-24 despite higher education. removing barriers to women's participation in the labour market means, of course, setting up a sufficient number of public or private childcare facilities for young children, at least until they enter compulsory schooling. it is also about applying equal parental rights in practice. for all these improvements in everyday life, the european union must defend the best standards, look upwards and the success of the scandinavian countries in gender equality measures.

on behalf of the pse group. madam president, this dossier is part of the dynamic that wants the european union to stand out in terms of animal welfare compared to its competitors. i would like to begin by congratulating the rapporteur, mr thijs berman, who has tried to strike a balance between positions which are too different. it is true that the emphasis has been on one criterion only, that of density. it is a pity that the commission proposal advocates the automatic recording of certain data, which seems difficult to envisage and to absorb by small producers. but let's go back to density, the criterion of all the challenges. a first economic approach in the walloon region shows that for a building of 1 000 square metres, on the basis of an average income of 72 euro cents per kilo, the loss of annual income would be estimated at 55% for a density of 30 kg per square metre and 24% for a density of 38 kg per square metre. this estimated loss of revenue is unbearable for the sector. the planned disappearance of many producers in europe and stagnating or rising consumer demand will necessarily benefit imports from countries that are far from applying or even considering our standards. animal welfare would lose out across the board. so while i am sensitive to the rapporteur's arguments for moving european agriculture towards sustainable agriculture, i would like to end by highlighting two fundamental elements. firstly, the move towards higher quality comes at an additional cost to european producers. this difference should be labelled and highlighted in a way decisive role to play in this respect. secondly, and finally, it is essential that we do our utmost to ensure that the european commission, with the help of international organisations such as the council of europe or the world organisation for animal health, succeeds in imposing greater acceptance of animal welfare measures by the wto.
president. - i have three comments on the human rights clause. first, it should be noted that the clause is rarely invoked: only 12 times since 1995. if we compare this with the number of non-democratic states with which the european union concludes treaties, we have to conclude that the clause is not used in practice for the most part. a second comment concerns the immigration and asylum policies of the member states, which the rapporteur wants to link to that human rights clause. this seems to me unrealistic and certainly undesirable, especially if third world countries are invited to criticise the reception of their own nationals seeking asylum in europe. we should make it a point to encourage such states to take back citizens who are not eligible for political asylum in our country. in practice, the opposite is often true. finally, it is not desirable to involve non-governmental organisations in the evaluation of the application of the human rights clause in relation to third countries. such political responsibility should not be given to organisations which have no democratic legitimacy whatsoever. europe needs to be more accountable for what happens to taxpayers' money.
in writing. i have of course voted in favour of the directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to optical radiation. this text is a continuation of three previous directives which were also designed to protect workers against the dangers of various 'physical agents' such as exposure to noise, vibration and electromagnetic fields. this directive mainly concerns radiation sources such as lasers or infrared lamps. it provides for adaptation of working methods in order to reduce the risks of radiation, the duration and the level of exposure. it also instructed the european commission to draw up a practical guide for employers, and in particular smes. this last measure deserves to be highlighted, because i believe that the union must do more for smes, which are the real spearheads of innovation and job creation in the europe of twenty-five! this applicable legislation combines flexibility for the employer and security for the employees. it is therefore a concrete example that, contrary to the ideas conveyed by neo-euro-sceptics who are experts in the no, the european union cares about workers, salaried workers and others, from the west and the east, and often advocates the levelling down of the social legislation of the member states.
i fully agree with the two previous speakers. it is true that the degree of civilization of a society is partly determined by the way in which people in that civilization treat animals, but i am also convinced that farmers, in this case the chicken farmers in our countries, can be accused of anything but cruelty or insensitivity to animals. i would like to point out, however, that our european companies are facing unfair competition from meat imports from non-european countries, such as brazil and thailand, where the standards are totally different, for example, in terms of occupancy, food safety, traceability, animal feed and antibiotics. the european broiler farmers certainly do not want to go back to the days when everything was permissible and they are prepared to breed in an animal- and environmentally-friendly way, but then we must also demand and check that all imported meat meets the same standards. unfortunately, that is not the case today. in fact, we are sadly leaving our own european growers behind.
in writing. is improving the welfare of chickens a topic worth debating in a parliamentary chamber? does it have a european dimension to justify fifty minutes of debate on chickens for meat production? these are two questions which are in themselves a starting point for an answer. it is not the first time that the european parliament has expressed its opinion on a subject which is far from the concerns of the citizens. however, i do not think that the european parliament has any interest in adopting the slogan of a "all-in-one europe" and, on the contrary, i am convinced that it has an interest in putting a stop to the regulatory frenzy. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's fisheries policy is not disrupted by the introduction of new fishing methods. these are just a few of the many proposals that are equally uninspiring. in conclusion, europe has nothing to gain from this kind of regulation. she might leave feathers!
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on employment and social affairs. mr commissioner, mr president-in-office, ladies and gentlemen, first of all i should like to congratulate my colleague, evelyne gebhardt, on her hard work so far. i am simply in awe of her. i would also like to thank the colleagues from the other groups. i think the work we have done in the last few weeks is indeed due to see the light of day. there was no working in a closed circle or in a circle, there was every possibility for open discussion. i would also like to thank my colleagues in the committee on employment and social affairs and say that we are indeed sending an important signal, because parliament now has an important responsibility to assume. we must prove that it is possible to create an internal market for services which fully realises our social model and thus vote a directive which has nothing to do with social dumping. that is one of the signals given by the committee on employment and social affairs, ladies and gentlemen. the signals given are, i think, accepted by the people out here in the streets of strasbourg, but also by a large number of our colleagues. firstly, full respect for labour legislation, social protection, collective agreements and industrial relations, including collective action: i think there is enough material on the table for this parliament to be able to rely on to fully guarantee this. secondly, my committee said that the services directive should under no circumstances undermine existing european social legislation. the second directive is of course particularly sensitive in this respect. the services directive is often seen as a license to reduce the working conditions and wages of workers and in some countries, in many countries, there have been incidents of recent times due to malpractice. i can't help but think of laval in sweden, struik foods in belgium, irish ferries and you name it. in most cases, ladies and gentlemen, these are simply illegal practices, but the services directive threatened to make controls even more difficult, and so it is important that we make it clear that the services directive remains fully valid and that articles 24 and 25 are deleted. that's not the end of the story. yes, commissioner mccreevy, we will have to take measures to make it easier for bona fide employers to access information and to counteract bureaucratic measures, but, commissioner, we will also have to take measures to ensure that the law on secondment, which is a good law in theory, is also respected in practice. there is too much cycling around this directive, too much abuse. thirdly, ladies and gentlemen, my committee has made it clear that we really must do something about the scope of the directive. it is good that there is already a consensus to exclude social services, health services, temporary employment offices and security services from this directive, because they have a completely different logic. my committee actually felt that all services of general economic interest should not be included here, for the simple reason that they have no commercial purpose but that the primary purpose is to provide a service of public interest, i.e. to guarantee basic human rights. i think it would be a good thing if we could get it out. i can be brief on the principle of the country of origin, mr commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the committee on employment and social affairs considered that this principle was unacceptable. i think the compromise on the table is a good one, because it allows the countries where the service is provided to still take legislative action in the public interest. in conclusion, ladies and gentlemen, i hope that on thursday the european parliament will be able to send a strong signal to both the commission and the council with a new, completely amended text on the radical social adjustment of the original commission text which this european parliament wants, because only then will we be able to convince public opinion that this text from the european parliament is not a license for social dumping.
madam president, i would like to join in the thanks to evelyne gebhardt and anne van lancker for their excellent work, but also for the loyalty and the spirit of cooperation they have shown to my group and to the others. that said, after hearing malcom harbour speak after anne and evelyne, i am not really sure that the famous common text is the same for everyone, which worries me and confirms the idea expressed by heide rühle that it may be a compromise that increases legal insecurity instead of reducing it. mr mccreevy, president barroso has spoken of a misunderstanding. it is clear that the misunderstanding is partly due to this famous principle of the country of origin. we all want jobs to be created, but in order for jobs to be created, it is not enough to increase competition, it is not enough for prices to fall so that demand is expressed. demand is expressed when people have confidence, citizens and workers alike. to have confidence, they must be convinced of the fairness of the competition rules. that is why the principle of country of origin not only does not exist in the treaties legally, but would, in my view, be economically disastrous. we do not want a union of 25 in which there is competition between national rules and national rules. if we want to send positive political signals to the citizens of the new member states, let us have a budget commensurate with the solidarity we must show. let us ensure, as the commission has advised - and i say this to the presidency of the council - that all the member states lift the restrictions on the free movement of workers. let us make sure that 1 may 2006 is indeed 1 may for all workers in the 25 countries of this european union. finally, mr mccreevy, during the previous parliamentary term, a majority of the members of the european parliament speaking in plenary asked the commission twice for a proposal for a framework directive on services of general interest, or public services, to avoid these services being subject to the jurisprudence of the court of justice or to derogations from competition law. what are you waiting for to make us this offer? that was an electoral commitment we had made: to work on a framework directive on services of general interest and a directive on the internal market for services in parallel. you yourself, your attitude, your commission, by not doing so, create an imbalance, create or reinforce the distrust felt by a fraction of the citizens. in this respect, you do not support the creation of a union of 25 under the banner of solidarity between east and west and of equality for all workers.
the proposal for a services directive was extremely controversial when it was first introduced, including among the flemish christian democrats. we felt that its scope was too broad and its deregulation drive too strong, that it did not respect the division of powers between the member states sufficiently and that the proposal left too many ambiguities. all this contributed to widespread fear of the impact of this directive, which has been used and abused and has gradually become the symbol of the gap with the citizens. but at the same time we have always been aware that the completion of the internal market in services is a necessity and can make a tangible contribution to our prosperity. if we as a parliament want to take responsibility for the strategy for growth and jobs, a good services directive is our instrument of choice. we cannot leave behind a potential of 600 000 jobs. that is why we have never chosen rejection and have opted for recasting from the outset and have so far been quite successful in our approach. the fact that you read the texts and put them in context confirms this. ladies and gentlemen, the vote in the imco committee has given us as members of parliament a direction in the debate. we have shown that the proposal can be amended in such a way that the objective of eliminating red tape and irresponsible obstacles to free movement is achievable without affecting social protection. i am particularly proud of the result that our group, led by malcolm harbour, has been able to produce in the imco committee. with the help of the uen event of the insolvency of their employer is a very good one. we look forward to the proposals announced by the commission and i hope that we will achieve our objective on thursday.

president. - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, after two and a half years of intensive research and inspections, the international atomic energy agency cannot confirm the peaceful nature of the iranian nuclear programme and very important questions remain unanswered. the recent iranian decision to resume uranium enrichment activities and also to cease all voluntary cooperation with the agency is particularly worrying, especially in the light of the statements by the iranian president denying israel's right to exist, as well as the general tension to be resolved in iran. the referral of the matter to the security council is accelerating the process, even though it has been decided to wait for the next report to be presented by mr el barradei on 6 march. the stakes are too high. the non-proliferation of nuclear weapons and weapons of mass destruction is on the line. the stability of the whole region is at stake, as is the effectiveness of the international atomic energy agency itself. i rarely use strong language, but i can tell you that this issue is putting the entire international legal order under pressure. even if the conference on the revision of the non-proliferation treaty has failed, which is very regrettable, the permanent members of the un security council, china, russia and the others, you know them, are today united with the european union in preventing proliferation by peaceful means, and that is indeed a matter of modest satisfaction. it is now up to iran, ladies and gentlemen, in full transparency and renewed cooperation with the agency, to convince the world that it has only peaceful aims in mind.

mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, after having analysed the report by mrs mcguinness, whose work i welcome, i would like to highlight a few points. firstly, it is our responsibility to ensure that the european union's rural strategy is developed and constructed as the explicit reference for any community policy or programme affecting rural areas. unfortunately, the commission's proposal limits its ambition to the implementation of the future rural development regulation and therefore does not allow for the establishment of bridges between rural, regional or social development policies. in reference to the lisbon strategy, the rural strategy should, mr president, madam commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, make a greater contribution to socioeconomic and territorial cohesion for all rural populations. the territorial conditions must be enabled and supported, in particular by creating or strengthening networks for the exchange of knowledge and improving the living conditions of rural populations. these conditions should not, moreover, encourage migration from cities to the countryside. finally, mr president, i think that we must encourage citizen participation and stimulate citizenship. these issues are of major importance for the construction of a democratic territorial identity. in conclusion, the commission should establish a dialogue area with the existing european networks and support their operation, while preserving their independence. this would help to boost relations with the actors on the ground.
mr president, if i think it useful, for the first time, to justify my vote, it is both because this report is important, perhaps the most important of the legislature, and because my vote is in breach of the majority position of my group. i have no difficulty in admitting that progress has been made - and i congratulate the rapporteur, mrs gebhardt - compared with commissioner bolkestein's initial proposal. i am, however, still concerned about the uncertainties surrounding article 16. although i voted in favour of the compromise amendments on this report, i cannot endorse it and have chosen to reject it in the end, because it represents a serious historical break with the principles which have, up to now, helped to advance the construction of europe. solidarity and regulations give way to competition between the countries and peoples of europe. i'm sorry to hear that.
the fact is, mr president, that the text on the services directive which we have just adopted is much better than the original version. in particular, the principle of the country of origin, which is unacceptable to me, has not yet been completely abolished, but it has already been weakened considerably. at the same time, the text we have adopted today also contains a great many ambiguities. no man at this time really knows the correct implication of a great many things. i am not yet convinced, for example, that this text cannot lead to social dumping and i am particularly concerned about the capacity to monitor its application. the numerous and even institutionalized abuses in the implementation of normal european budgets, for example, may well make us doubt the efficiency of control at european level. i therefore abstained from voting because this is a step in the right direction and a very important one, but there are still too many ambiguities which must be removed from the text by the second reading. i can only hope that i shall be able to vote in favour without any problems on the second reading.
the amendments made today in this half-hour debate to the original text of the services directive are, of course, a good thing. the commission has already made a number of proposals. the directive in its original form indeed institutionalized social dumping, but the current text still allows abuse and a form of social dumping and does not establish in unambiguous terms the primacy of the country of destination. is it not illustrative that the establishment of the observatory requested by the uen group to ensure that the directive does not lead to social dumping has been rejected? our abstention is therefore a political warning that my party, vlaams belang, will, as it did last month when discussing the liberalization of port services, oppose the undermining of the principle of working for one's own people first.
mr president, my negative vote on the gebhardt report does not mean that i am dismissing the extraordinary work that the rapporteur has done. i really think that with a terrible text, evelyne gebhardt has done something. but it has not been able, despite all its efforts, to stem a liberal wave which is threatening our entire social fabric. from compromise to compromise, this obscure text has become unreadable. but what is very clear to anyone who wants to open their eyes is the abandonment of a desire for harmonization on the part of the commission in favour of a case-by-case regulation of what can or cannot be done in the name of the general interest. without a directive on services of general interest and services of general economic interest, this text leaves grey areas which will open the way to countless legal remedies. europe is moving further away from the citizen.
in writing. i voted against the report on the services directive in the final vote for three reasons. the improvements made by parliament to the commission's draft are not, in my view, sufficient. a number of sectors of the public service, the so-called services of general economic interest, are unjustly left out of the scope of the directive. for example, garbage collection and water distribution are not covered by this service directive. the right to public services should also be enshrined in a framework law. (2) several parts of the directive can be interpreted in an ambiguous way. there is a scepticism in public opinion about europe today, and so legal uncertainty about legislation can be seen as a toothache. 3) this is not the way to build a social and reliable europe with the same harmonised rules of the game throughout the internal market. i am glad, however, that several sensitive sectors such as port services, the temporary employment sector, elderly care, health care and childcare have been excluded from the service and that the social achievements of each country are preserved, so that social dumping is not possible. i have supported the amendments on this, as well as all the amendments which improved the text and brought more clarity.
in writing. the adoption of the services directive is a significant step forward for the european union. the main elements of the initial draft were nevertheless maintained and the text adopted will contribute to the success of the lisbon strategy, as the most recent studies of the european commission show. this directive will encourage activity in the service sector, which is a very important source of employment. this will be a key asset, especially for belgium, whose economy is strongly service-oriented. the amendments introduced by the committee on the internal market have addressed the main concerns of public opinion by clarifying the scope of the directive and putting an end to a long campaign of disinformation. the first epp-pse compromise proposal stripped the proposal for a directive of all meaning. fortunately, the final compromise - even if it does not evoke enthusiasm - is a first step which is better than nothing and meets the main concerns of the proponents of the directive. it is now up to the council to confirm and improve the final compromise reached by parliament.
in writing. the european parliament has certainly made history today in european parliamentary democracy by voting on the so-called bolkestein directive. by reconciling the irreconcilable, the position of france and poland, that of the etuc and unice, that of the progressive socialists and the liberal moderates, this vote represents a step forward towards a europe of citizens. a european union which does not compromise with social dumping but does not forget the removal of protectionist barriers to the free provision of services and the freedom of establishment. yes, our parliament is coming out of this vote in a big way. not only has it fully fulfilled its role as legislator - for example by abolishing the principle of country of origin - but it has also avoided the trap set by a coalition of eurosceptics and frivolous non-nons who, struck with amnesia, seem to have forgotten that on 1 june 2004 we were celebrating european reconciliation. by favouring the policy of extending a hand to our friends in the ten new member states, we have simply brought down a new berlin wall. in our heads this time, and for a long time i hope!
in writing. anyone who has followed the debate on the services directive closely will be surprised to find that the compromise between the conservatives and the social democrats is interpreted in different ways. one group on the right defends the compromise as "not affecting the principle of the country of origin principle", while the left sees it as "a final end to the same country of origin principle". so, you're saying that this is a flagrant contradiction. the compromise also does not clarify the extent to which member states may impose strict conditions on the provision of certain services in their territory and thus prevent social dumping. the epp and pse have also dropped the reference to social policy and consumer protection. it is a good thing that parliament has succeeded in excluding a whole range of services from the scope of the directive. on the other hand, services of general economic interest are still largely covered by the directive. the revised bolkestein directive contains a great deal of legal ambiguity and will therefore again cause a great deal of litigation before the european court of justice. the revised text certainly does not provide the necessary transparency and legal certainty. the greens were particularly concerned about the exclusion of services of general economic interest and the removal of the country of origin principle from the final text. our wishes on this point have not been met. that is why i voted against it in the end.

the rapporteur. mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the subject of my report, the relocation of companies, is a delicate but nonetheless topical one. i immediately perceived this delicate aspect when i presented my first draft report to the committee on regional development. i have rarely seen such a passionate debate in committee, although i am still a young member of parliament. the fact remains that the clash of ideas often brings about a light and i think we have arrived at a well balanced text, which sets out several interesting ideas and which allows us to demystify, clarify and better understand the complex mechanisms of a real social problem. i would also like to take this opportunity to thank the fictitious rapporteurs and all the colleagues who have contributed to the preparation of this report. ladies and gentlemen, many of us in this chamber, perhaps not tonight but in general, have seen that relocations often have significant economic consequences for the region affected. but relocation also means, and this affects us, job losses and tragedy for the families who are victims. the weakness of the european statistical tool in this area is a first preliminary observation i was given to make when preparing this report. i have also noted that this house has already dealt with this matter in other reports. in a first resolution of 13 march 2003 on the closure of firms which have received financial aid from the european union, parliament pointed to the major danger posed by relocations to the primary objective of regional policy, namely economic and social cohesion. then there was the resolution of 6 july last by our colleague mr hatzidakis on the reform of the structural funds. on that occasion parliament made several proposals on relocations. my report, of course, takes up the ideas contained in the resolutions previously adopted by the european parliament: i have tried to reproduce the essential principles of those resolutions. after recalling that the objective of regional development policy is to promote the development of the regions of the european union and that public aid should not be used to encourage the relocation of economic activities, the report voted by our committee on regional development proposes that measures be adopted, both at community and national level, to prevent the potential negative consequences of relocations on economic development and the social disasters generated by them. first, we ask for a tool to give us access to more accurate information. we therefore propose to entrust the dublin observatory with the task of studying, assessing and monitoring the phenomenon of relocations in order to objectively assess their impact on the economic, social and cohesion and regional development policies. in addition to the tool, and in the absence of better coordination of our national social systems, the committee on regional development believes that it has become imperative to adopt a comprehensive european strategy for preventing, supervising and monitoring the relocation of companies within the union but also outside it. we propose a number of measures which we address very specifically in the report. firstly, we ask the commission to take all the necessary measures to prevent - and i believe this is a key element of this report - european regional policy from being an incentive to relocation. secondly, we support the commission's proposal to penalize companies which have received aid from the european union and relocate their activities within seven years. we also ask that, in the event of non-compliance with national and international legislation, these same companies should no longer be able to receive public aid for their new place of business. we ask the commission to consider measures in respect of relocations which i regard as reverse relocations, i.e. those which lead to a deterioration in employment conditions without relocating the business of the company. we believe that the granting and maintenance of public aid to enterprises must be subject to specific commitments in the field of employment and local development. we suggest that the commission should develop a mechanism to impose more severe penalties on publicly supported companies which relocate all or part of their activities outside the european union. we also call on the commission to include social clauses in international treaties and to support the implementation of these social clauses with positive measures and incentives for countries and companies that comply with them. we support the commission's proposal to create a globalisation fund to prevent and deal with the economic and social shocks resulting from restructuring and relocation. finally, the committee on regional development believes that the consequences of the many relocations must lead us to open and constructive reflection on the question of creating a genuine european social area. in this respect, we believe that social dialogue has a major role to play in preventing relocations and in addressing their effects. ladies and gentlemen, as mr president, to be clear, i voted against the gröner report not because i am against equality between men and women, far from it, but because this report and the gender equality institute it contains (what a word!) illustrate the suffocating political correctness that europe is increasingly embracing. the charter of fundamental rights, a politically correct left-wing policy document enforced by the equally politically correct fundamental rights office, the job quotas and the other measures proposed in the dead but reappearing european constitution all fall within the same ideology. let us for the love of god drop these things and concentrate on the really important issue, namely that women have the right to be involved in the whole labour process on an equal footing with men and to receive equal pay for equal work. we all agree on that. everything else is just political correctness.
in writing. by adopting my colleague marios matsakis' report on the community strategy on mercury on tuesday, the european parliament has sent a strong signal to the international community: the most toxic chemicals must be strictly controlled and europe of the twenty-five must lead by example by prohibiting their export quickly. this is the lot of the future for mercury, a heavy metal which is toxic to humans and ecosystems and of which europe is the world's largest exporter. i particularly welcome the adoption of paragraph 17 which proposes to limit the use of mercury in dental amalgams by the end of 2007. parliament thus confirms its vote of 25 january 2005 on my report on the european environment and health action plan, which in point 6 proposed the use of safer alternatives to mercury used in dental amalgams. it was common sense that won out here. human exposure should be kept to a minimum. it is therefore equally essential that the european union quickly finds a solution to the treatment of the 12 000 tonnes of mercury waste that will be produced over the next 15 years by the chlorine and soda industry.
on behalf of the alde group. - mr president, commissioner, recent figures from your services, which you have apparently known for a long time, have taught us that since the liberalisation of european imports last year, monthly imports of shoes from china have increased by 400% and in some cases by as much as 900%. you have just announced dumping duties. some say "too little and too late". they believe you could have prevented something by being more alert, by reacting more quickly. the european footwear industry is a relatively small sector, limited to four european countries and even largely localised. the question is whether this is a sufficient reason to let this sector disappear altogether, especially if it has to fight - and you yourself have said that - against unfair trade practices such as dumping or direct and hidden state aid. the free market today is a socially corrected market where there are global rules of the game which must be observed by every participant. this is clearly not the case here. china wants the benefits of who membership, but forgets that there are obligations that come with it. it is clear that this threatens to disrupt the entire world market. yesterday textiles, today shoes, what will it be tomorrow? why, commissioner, was there no response earlier? or is the commission really afraid of facing the big yellow giant china? and above all, can the commissioner explain to us how we should explain the benefits of this kind of globalisation to the workers in the european footwear industry, who are now in danger of losing their jobs as a result of unfair competition?

president, the lisbon strategy must ensure that europe becomes the strongest economy in the world. i think that is an objective on which there is a great deal of consensus in this european parliament. although i do have some questions about the way in which this is being achieved. i can only say that today's europe is still predominantly concerned with subsidies and far too little with investment and innovation. the european commission, as well as the member states, has a heavy responsibility in this area. while the commission had proposed to double spending on science and development to €10 billion a year from 2007, this was blocked by the european council, as it would require significant cuts in agricultural subsidies, regional subsidies and also in the structural funds. when i think of the european structural funds, i automatically think of the bottomless pit of wallonia, for example, into which millions of euros are pouring every year without any structural change because it is blocked by a pervasive and corrupt socialist party. this is now confirmed by all kinds of welsh politicians and leading economists. then someone like mrs danuta hübner, the european commissioner for regional policy, comes along and says that wallonia is making good use of the structural funds and that the walloon projects are indicative of the so-called remarkable structural changes taking place in that region. however, coming from someone who is partly responsible for the follow-up to the lisbon strategy, this raises some questions.

in writing. i voted in favour of this report, which encourages more environmentally friendly fishing methods. the priority now is to reduce the intensity of fishing activity in order to allow the recovery of stocks. we know this is a sensitive matter, but it's urgent. indeed, 46% of the 28,000 fish species worldwide are threatened. furthermore, the united nations ecosystem assessment programme points out that 25 percent of commercial species are overexploited. the social and economic imperatives must of course be taken into account and the fisheries sector, which is already subject to too many constraints, must not be penalized. reduction of fishing activity may be considered if it is linked to compensation. but other measures can bring significant results, such as strengthening the fight against pollution from ships or promoting sustainable fishing methods. the sustainability of fisheries resources is a key objective and it is in this spirit that i have oriented my report for opinion on the commission communication on a community approach to eco-labelling programmes for fishery products.

problem of the budget is again a similar one: a reduced budget means reduced policies and, again, it means less money for the union of 27 – 25 plus 2 – than we had for the 15. in the age of consumer protection, where we have to take strong initiatives, especially in the new member states and the acceding member states, this will cause serious problems and will of course affect the assistance we offer to consumer organisations, especially as regards funding of projects and training of staff. on the same issue, i would like to remind you again of the letter sent by president barroso to health area, if eventually we are left with such a major cut or reduction in our funding, it will mean that we cannot spread it thinly over many actions. limited funds mean we have to re-evaluate and prioritise and decide where we actually want to concentrate, where we can achieve the greatest benefit. i hope that this can be corrected, that eventually the funding will be provided and that we will be able to cover the programme as it was proposed. on splitting the programmes, i have to repeat that i fully understand the position of the committee on the internal market and consumer protection in this respect. i understand why it would prefer to have two separate programmes. as i said earlier, we believe that there would be some added value if we adhered to a merged programme, where we clearly distinguish between actions in health and in consumer protection; but we can benefit from having common areas and achieve better economies of scale. nevertheless, given that the financial perspectives negotiations are still ongoing, this would and will affect the commission’s position on the splitting of the programme. at this point, therefore, we cannot take a final decision regarding splitting and have to reject the amendments relating to splitting. on the conclusion of the debate on the financial perspectives, the commission will review this issue again. i repeat that parliament has made its wishes very clear and i have taken note of the very strong – almost unanimous – position of parliament. i shall not comment on the amendments separately. a full list of the commission’s position on each of the amendments is being made available to parliament and i should be obliged if it could be included in the verbatim report of this sitting commission’s position on amendments by parliament: see annex.. the amendments we are rejecting relate mostly to the splitting, and we are doing so either for the reason i explained earlier or because they go beyond the scope of community consumer policy. it is not that we disagree with the substance of these proposals, but that we feel they go a bit further than the scope of community consumer policy and – given the existing situation – we would not be able to finance some of them. we agree on the other issues, especially as regards the need to integrate consumer interests into other policies. i count on parliament’s support to ensure that consumer interests are given proper attention in many other key consumer policy initiatives. that is a brief overview of the commission’s position on this issue. i look forward to a very interesting debate.
