in writing. today, parliament gave its opinion on the strategy for future enlargements of the european union. the enlargements "reunited" with our european brothers, bulgarians, romanians, croats, macedonians and others, arbitrarily separated from us after yalta. they are destined to join us as much as the union is destined to integrate them. i am not contesting the substance, but the form and timing, and that is why i voted for paragraphs 5 and 6. it is time for the union to have a real debate on its own borders, something it has carefully avoided doing during the work of the convention. a black hole in the constitution that has fueled widespread skepticism and concern. avoiding what is unpleasant is not an attitude worthy of us and our voters, and asking for a debate does not make us refuzniks of enlargement! a europe without frontiers does not serve those who want a europe of power. our borders will be geographical, historical and moral, but they are necessary to preserve a model, a project, common values.
i would like to thank the rapporteur for his report, which is a very good report, and i would like to thank the rapporteur for his report, which is a very good report, and i would like to thank the rapporteur for his report. i believe that if we are to build a genuine european parliamentary democracy, genuine european political parties are an important part of that. the decision taken a few years ago to finance the parties as such, apart from the political groups, seems to me to be a very important step in that direction. this step is not necessarily popular with the population, because the population does not think it is necessary. i think, however, that we have a duty to point out that in a good parliamentary democracy political parties are an important link between the citizen and decision-making and that this also comes at a price. in other words, we must also dare to say out loud that democracy has a price, that this is not unusual and that funding should be made more transparent, with public funds rather than through all sorts of obscure channels. an important step in this direction was taken, as already mentioned, a few years ago. i think that now was the time to make some improvements, the most important of which was undoubtedly the structural one, which was to enable the political parties to plan for several years rather than always working with a budget for one year and being uncertain about what will happen the following year. there are also two important technical amendments, which relate to the flexibility with which the budget can be managed. i should like to take advantage of this opportunity to point out that the problem of transferring any reserves or bonuses which you have in a given year to the next is not confined to political parties. it seems to be a rule that has been dropped somewhere in the general rules imposed by the commission. as president of the governing board of the college of europe, i am faced with the same problem: again, you have to have spent the money in a certain year, otherwise you have a problem and that leads to poor management. there should be more flexibility on that point, as we are proposing here for the political party. i would draw the commissioner's attention to the fact that this problem is not confined to the political party. it seems to be a general rule applied by the commission which actually hinders good management and has been devised by accountants whom i do not understand. finally, i would also like to stress that we can still do important work with the committee on constitutional affairs on a genuine statute for political parties, which - even if it is to the detriment of my own country's treasury - should also include a fiscal statute that is more in line with the fiscal statute of the european institutions, so that it becomes very clear that political parties belong to that specific level of government, that they fit seamlessly into the institutional framework of europe.
the leinen report, mr president, is based on the belief that full-fledged european political parties are necessary because they are a real benefit to democracy at european level. i think it's fair to say that the opposite is true. the unrestrained expansion of the decision-making process in a europe which must be made up of very diverse and very sovereign member states is the surest way to less democracy and even less transparent decision-making through a bureaucracy which can hardly be controlled, and there are already many examples of this today, for example the totalitarian way in which the rejection of the european constitution in democratic referendums in france and the netherlands is dealt with in this parliament. the european political parties must once again be used to divert masses of taxpayers' money to yet another additional party funding, and then only for politically correct parties. so when europe talks about democracy, we better watch our counts.
i have abstained on the vote on the bushill-matthews report because i cannot agree with several of the recommendations. i am thinking in particular of recital 70 on immigration policy, although i must admit in parentheses that even that recital 70 on immigration policy contains a formulation which is strangely nuanced for this parliament. it must be said that this report is actually quite balanced and at least has the merit of putting the demographic problem of europe back on the agenda. what i miss in this report is the observation that very many parents today still choose voluntarily to work at home and for their own family and that probably many more people would choose this path if it were made financially feasible by the government. my party has long advocated full parental pay, including social security and the building up of pension rights for the home-working parent with minor children. i am convinced that this measure could also make a considerable contribution to the enormous demographic challenge we are facing.
in writing. i voted in favour of the bushill-matthews report on demographic challenges and solidarity between generations. the fact is that europe is ageing. in my country, the equation is simple: because we live in a system of legal pension distribution that requires each generation of workers to pay the pensions of the generations that worked before them, this means very concretely that fewer workers will have to finance the pensions and health care of a higher number of pensioners, who will live longer and longer. if nothing is done, either future generations will have to bear much heavier burdens at the expense of their own living standards, or if they refuse to do so, tomorrow's pensioners will find themselves gradually reduced to a subsistence standard of living and rationed health care. clearly, neither of these two possibilities is acceptable. none of them will be accepted. we must find other ways, and urgently: all the countries of the union are facing this challenge. it is the honour of a democracy to anticipate the response to the challenges it knows are inevitable. we owe it to future generations of europeans.
in writing. on the occasion of the adoption of this report, which i supported, i would like to address the recent developments in bioenergy in belgium. in fact, in addition to micro-economic projects relating to the use of biofuels on farms, large-scale industrial projects are finally being set up in my country, and i am delighted about this. as regards production, six major projects are currently underway, the two largest being located in ghent (biodiesel) and wanze (bioethanol). in addition, the biofuel tax exemption programme adopted by belgium and approved by the european commission sets the blending rate for biodiesel with traditional diesel at 3,37% for 2006 and 4,29% for 2007. for bioethanol the mixture was set at 7%. at this rate, will we even reach the target set in directive 2003/30 of 5.75% of all fuels sold being 'green' by 2010? in this respect, the parish report calls for these quantified targets to be made mandatory. the energy-saving use of agricultural products, a real opportunity for the future of our farmers, which respects the environment, merits the setting of binding objectives, especially if they are reasonable.

member of the commission. mr president, when we debated this proposal at the february part-session i said that the commission would base its modified proposal on what parliament voted. i believe you will see that we have honoured that commitment in the text adopted by the college today. we have endeavoured to respect the consensus you have reached on some of the key articles. you will find the amendments you have voted to article 16 – freedom to provide services – and article 17 – derogations from freedom to supply services – reflected in the modified proposal. we have excluded all healthcare from the scope of the proposal and, in line with the commitment given, we will come forward with a separate initiative on the health sector. the modified proposal also provides for the exclusion of taxation, temporary work agencies, security services and the audiovisual sector from the scope of the proposal. one issue on scope where we did not follow you is the exclusion of legal services. the commission considers this is not required given that article 3 already provides that in case of conflict between a specific directive and this proposal it is the former that will prevail. in addition, we have brought the wording on the exercise of an official authority in line with article 45 of the ec treaty. we have based the exclusion of social services on the various amendments you adopted in this regard. i am sure you will agree that any exclusion must be clearly defined, and this is what we have attempted to do. if we want to avoid diverging interpretation in member states, then we must provide legal certainty. the text of the modified proposal specifies that the exclusion from the directive concerns those social services relating to social housing, children and support to families and persons in need which are provided by the state or by providers mandated by the state. in addition, the commission will come forward in the next few weeks with a communication on social services of general interest, reflecting the importance these services have for our citizens the decision to remove all interaction between the services proposal and labour law has in my opinion been one of the most important elements in creating a more positive atmosphere around this new services proposal. this has allowed us to move on from the allegations of lowering of social standards and threats to the european social model. while this perception was wrong, it did not go away, and poisoned the debate on this important proposal. in any event, the modified proposal is clear in this regard: labour law is completely removed. as part of the fallout from this, articles 24 and 25 have been removed from the modified proposal. however, as i said during the debate in february, the commission would come forward with a communication to address the issues surrounding the posting of workers, which articles 24 and 25 were designed to address. in a moment my colleague vladimir špidla will outline to you the approach the commission adopted today. i just want to add that this communication is a vital element in our efforts to reach agreement in the council on the services proposal. there are two other issues in the modified proposal that i want to highlight. we have clarified in article 3 that all special laws take precedence over the provisions of the services proposal. in particular, we have made it clear in article 3 that the directive does not affect private international law and that, as a consequence, consumers will in principle benefit from the protection granted to them by the consumer protection rule laid down in the consumer legislation in force in their member state. the commission has also accepted that the professional liability insurance in article 27 should be optional instead of compulsory. since the european parliament voted in february, there has been a growing realisation throughout the european union that the consensus you achieved was the basis for taking this debate forward. heads of government at the european council two weeks ago supported this. we have a window of opportunity for building on this growing consensus. i want to see the potential for growth and jobs that this proposal will bring realised as soon as possible. i will now present this modified proposal to the informal competitiveness council in graz later this month. i feel confident that, with the active support of the austrian presidency and member states, we stand a real chance of making significant progress during this presidency. member of the commission. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the commission has just adopted a communication containing guidelines and clarifications for the member states on the posting of workers in the framework of the crossborder provision of services. the communication also contains proposals to help member states improve the practical functioning of administrative cooperation, access to information and supervision of compliance with the posting of workers directive. the commission already announced this communication last february, following the vote in the european parliament on the services directive, and announced at the time that it would follow the result of the majority vote on the repeal of articles 24 and 25, which contain provisions on administrative obstacles to the posting of workers. however, the omission of those articles should not be interpreted by member states as indicating that they may introduce or maintain excessive administrative barriers for undertakings that post workers abroad. cross-border posting of workers is governed by directive 96/71/ec, which aims to harmonise the right of a company to provide cross-border services on the one hand, and the rights of workers who are temporarily posted to another country to provide those services there, on the other. this directive is a key instrument to ensure the free movement of services and at the same time a key instrument to prevent social dumping. the legal clarification adopted by the commission today offers the necessary clarification on the control measures that member states may use to verify compliance with the working conditions laid down in the directive. the purpose is to ensure that states act in accordance with the principle of free movement of services, while avoiding social dumping. the communication offers clarifications and guidance on four types of control measures in particular: the requirement to obtain a permit, the obligation to have a representative on the territory of the host member state, the obligation to notify and the obligation to provide documents concerning social and working conditions. the conclusions of the communication are as follows: as regards the obligation to have a representative in the territory of the host member state, this obligation is considered to be somewhat excessive and is considered sufficient to entrust a worker e.g. a master with the task of liaising with the competent authorities of the member states. as regards the systematic prior notification obligation, it is not considered appropriate to apply this notification obligation solely because it is an outsourced service; as regards the documents to be retained, the obligation to retain documents is, of course, recognised, but it is assumed that these documents must be only those which are strictly necessary to enable the member state authorities to provide effective assistance and to carry out effective controls. this does not apply, for example, to documents relating to social security, since they are covered by another directive, namely directive 1408/71. it is further concluded that it is appropriate to require prior notification of the activity. the notification of the activity must take place at the latest on the day of the start of the activity itself. the commission also intends to make it easier for workers and businesses to access information on working conditions and to improve cooperation between national authorities. it is also necessary to improve the means available to workers and businesses to ensure effective enforcement of the rules on employees. on the basis of the commission's analysis of the implementation of the directive, which can be found in the commission's administration, we have concluded that there is still a lot of room for improvement in all areas and that the communication contains a number of proposals addressed to the member states in order to address these shortcomings; inter alia, improving the website and other information resources, strengthening the contact points and bodies responsible for monitoring the use of electronic systems for the exchange of information and improving contact between labour inspectorates. i consider it quite important that member states should indicate in their information the specific obligations to be met by the sending firm, rather than merely referring to the labour law generally applicable or to the legal system in force in the member state concerned. ladies and gentlemen, we are convinced that the posting of workers directive, if properly applied, is a sufficient and solid instrument to prevent social dumping on the one hand and to open up the field of freedom to provide services on the other. (applause) thank you very much.

the president of the council. i have to say, madam president, the fact that you are helping me to make a very practical contribution to better legislation by giving me the privilege of meeting the conference of committee chairmen and having a very intensive discussion of legislation is very much linked to this debate. anyway, thank you for your understanding. i should like to comment briefly on one or two subjects which have been raised in this debate. the question of comitology was raised by many speakers. in austria, they say, "don't cackle over eggs that haven't been laid". but we have just taken up the subject of comitology with great commitment under the austrian presidency. we have already had two very intensive rounds of negotiations with the representatives of the european parliament, the last of which was only about two hours ago. i think we are on the right track, after a few years, to reach a solution that is satisfactory to parliament and to the council. in any case, the present situation will improve, because parliament will naturally be involved in the legislative acts adopted by the council and parliament in accordance with the codecision procedure. we are relatively close to a solution here, and i hope that we can reach a final solution within this half-year. we are also contributing to better legislation. mrs frassoni has of course rightly stressed the importance of enforcement. this concerns the member states, not the council as such - and i am speaking here, of course, on behalf of the council - but the individual member states are naturally seeking, in discussions with the commission, to find better ways of helping the application of the law to make a breakthrough. we are also - and i say this as an austrian - affected by this, of course, as are all the other member states, and we are also affected by infringement proceedings, as are many other member states. as a lawyer, i say that every right obviously needs an enforcement mechanism; member of the commission. the honourable member will be aware that part of the procedure that any applicant country must go through is that it must ensure that the directives of the community are put into effect. that will apply equally to bulgaria and romania. the member is probably also aware that these recommendations stem from the financial action task force – fatf, as it is known – and most countries around the world are bound to implement the recommendations of that particular body. in the eu we have taken it upon ourselves, in a directive, to implement the recommendations, which has been done in the third money laundering directive. you can rest assured that bulgaria will be taking those measures into account in its legislation, as it is part of its deal to acquire full membership of the european union.
member of the commission. the president has pointed out that i am taking this question on behalf of my colleague, mrs kroes. the commission has significant powers to ensure that member states fully respect the internal market rules and do not create unlawful obstacles to cross-border mergers. if a member state violates internal market rules, the commission can launch an infringement procedure pursuant to article 226 of the ec treaty. this procedure may require a certain time, as the commission has, at two separate stages, to grant the member state the opportunity to express its view before introducing an action before the court of justice, which then has to take the final decision on the alleged infringement. where justified by the circumstances of the case, the commission can act more speedily by giving very short deadlines to member states in the pre-litigation phase and asking the court to grant interim measures. even in that case, the commission is obliged to take into account the observations of the member states, including late responses, as it is settled case-law that the proper conduct of their pre-litigation procedure constitutes an essential guarantee required by the ec treaty, not only in order to protect the rights of the member state concerned but also to ensure that any contentious procedure would have a clearly defined dispute as its subject-matter. moreover, in cases where a member state intervenes with regard to concentrations with a community dimension, the commission has special powers to adopt a decision under article 21 of regulation (ec) no 139/2004, known as the merger regulation. pursuant to that provision, the commission has the exclusive competence to assess concentrations with a community dimension. member states can adopt measures which could prohibit or prejudice de jure or de facto such operations only if, first, the measures in question protect the interests other than those taken into account by the merger regulation and, second, those measures are necessary and proportionate for the protection of interests compatible with ec law. public security, plurality of media and prudential rules are considered to be interests compatible with ec law. measures adopted in pursuit of those interests must in any event be necessary and proportionate, and the commission may seek information from the national authorities about the intended measures in order to verify this. the other interests must be communicated to the commission before the adoption of those measures. the commission must then decide, within 25 working days, whether the national measures are justified for the protection of an interest compatible with ec law. the commission considers that the abovementioned provisions can allow it, in a timely manner, to act against any unlawful protectionist measures adopted by member states. the commission is, moreover, firmly committed to use all the powers at its disposal to ensure full compliance with ec law.
member of the commission. i can understand the intent of the supplementary question put by the honourable member. yes, it is sometimes frustrating to have to wait for a considerable period of time before there is action. however, we are a community of laws and we must abide by settled law on this. the procedures must be followed. even in member states these types of actions take some time. although sometimes there are mechanisms to expedite the process in member states, and even in the community you can exercise rights to expedite the process, it is still pretty time consuming. so, i am afraid that we cannot rule by fiat or by dictat in this community. it would be an abuse of privilege. i do not think anybody would advocate that. on the other hand, it is sometimes frustrating that there is such a considerable period of time, but i do not see any other way around it. furthermore, i do not foresee any member state rushing to give the commission more immediate new powers or powers to expedite the process. we have to live by what is there.
member of the commission. mr president, mr member of the european parliament, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your support. the french law to which the honourable member's question relates has not yet been notified to the commission and the commission is therefore not prepared to comment in detail on that law. the objective of directive 2000/78, as the honourable member mentions, is indeed to prohibit discrimination in employment and occupation, in particular discrimination on grounds of age. however, the directive also provides for a number of exceptions to the general rule. let me quote directly from the directive. 'nevertheless, differences in treatment linked to age may be justified in certain circumstances and the appellant therefore invokes specific provisions which may vary according to the situation in the member states. it is therefore essential to distinguish between differences in treatment which are justified, in particular by legitimate employment policy, labour market and vocational training objectives and discrimination which must be prohibited.' encore une citation: "the setting up of special conditions of access to employment and vocational training, employment and work including conditions of dismissal and remuneration for young people, older workers and those with dependants, in order to promote their occupational integration or to ensure their protection". you can see that the situation is twofold from the point of view of the directive. the directive recognises different treatment where the reasons are legitimate and recognises different measures where the nature of those measures is proportionate to the objective pursued. i cannot comment in more detail on the current situation. the court of justice in luxembourg can, of course, only give a final opinion on any law which is notified.
member of the commission. mr president, you have cited one of the cases which is very important for assessing similar situations, because the directive does indeed presuppose that any measure is both legitimate and proportionate to its purpose. in the case you mentioned, concerning older workers, the court's ruling in luxembourg was based on the fact that the purpose was legitimate, the method of the measure was not proportionate, and therefore the measure was rejected. at the moment, as i said, because the law has not yet been notified, we do not know what it looks like, and even as i can follow the media reports, its shape is still not stabilized, it is impossible for me as a representative of the commission to express myself with greater precision.
member of the commission. the commission naturally monitors the labour markets of individual countries and the development of labour law. the green paper on the development of labour law in europe will shortly be published, which i hope will provide the basis for a very thorough discussion of the customs, methods and developments of labour law and industrial relations in the member states of the european union. otherwise, of course, the commission can only monitor and decide within the framework provided for by the treaty, and here it must be said that the treaty does not provide for direct links to labour law.
member of the commission. council regulation (ec) no 485/2005 to which the honourable member refers extends the possibility of withdrawal of fishing vessels from the community fishing fleet with public aid to vessels which can be transferred to the countries affected by the tsunami for the benefit of the fishing communities concerned. this possibility is granted on an exceptional basis, under certain conditions and only during a limited period. while several countries in the indian ocean whose fishing sectors were affected by the tsunami disaster enquired about the possibility of a transfer of vessels, only sri lanka submitted a formal request for vessels. it concerned 120 vessels with an overall length of 9 to 12 metres. the commission informed member states of the request and reminded them on several occasions of the obligation to report on the transfer of vessels. despite the unanimous adoption of the regulation by the council, all 20 member states which are engaged in maritime fisheries have now informed the commission that they have not identified any possibility of transfers of vessels in response to the sri lankan request. as the regulation sets a deadline of 30 june 2006, there are at present no more prospects of transfers of vessels under the scheme.

the content of the programme of the takkula report, mr president, reminds me of the arrogant, belligerent attitude of official europe after the referendums in france and the netherlands. the stupid french and the stupid dutch had not understood and had to be re-educated. the perfect representative of this arrogant elite is commissioner michel, who literally said that the citizen is not always right and that in such a case the politicians must pave the way. the present programme, 'citizens for europe', also fits into this dubious communication and information strategy. by pouring hundreds of millions of euros into all sorts of meaningless, politically correct projects and organisations, the commission claims to be bringing citizens closer to the union. it is not a little ironic that the text also refers to plan d, so-called for democracy, dialogue and debate, but in the same month that the plan was proposed, negotiations on turkey's accession were started, knowing full well that the majority of the population in europe is against it. this is the kind of hypocrisy that more and more europeans are resisting.
in writing. i of course support this report which aims to bring citizens closer to the european institutions, an objective, indeed a necessity in today's europe where scepticism is growing. the report sets out communication strategies, citizen projects of a transnational nature and, above all, a duty of 'active european memory', a federal initiative to commemorate the victims of the deportations and mass exterminations committed by the nazi and communist regimes. i voted against all attempts to water down the text, some of whom wanted to refer to other forms of totalitarianism in europe. the atrocities committed by these two regimes, which affected all the peoples of europe, are at the origin of the european construction, and it is essential not to trivialize them. beyond this initiative, our role is to inform, meet, dialogue, convince, make sure that every citizen knows how europe shapes their daily lives, strengthen the feeling of belonging to europe, fight against any form of nationalist retreat, make the added value of europe come to life and remind that this is the most beautiful idea of the 20th century. we must remember that while some of us are beginning to doubt, the rest of the world is dreaming of europe!
in writing. yes, of course, and in principle, to the opening of the labour markets in the territory of the fifteen to workers from eight new member states. in 2004, the uk, ireland and sweden opened their borders without restriction. two years later, a commission report defends this policy, supported by figures: lower than expected migration flows and filling the shortage of workers in certain sectors. but yes, because we cannot afford to be complacent about the transitional arrangements made in this area by a majority of member states. that is why i support the belgian federal government's position that certain conditions for access to the labour market should be maintained until 2009 at the latest. limited restrictions in space which do not apply to the selfemployed such as tradesmen, craftsmen and the liberal professions who have enjoyed full freedom of establishment since the enlargement. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's social policy is not distorted by the introduction of new forms of work organization. the 'unprepared' member states must be given time to put in place the necessary registration and control structures for the free movement of workers from wherever they come.
in writing. i support the öry report on the transitional arrangements restricting the free movement of workers in the labour markets of the european union, because i share the view that it is better for all member states, including belgium, to abolish the transitional measures. the three countries that have opened their borders have not experienced a mass migration flow. it has also been shown that transitional periods lead to a significant increase in undeclared work and pseudo-independence, which puts pressure on wages and exposes workers to poor working conditions and unfair conditions. restricting the free movement of workers from the new member states is discriminatory, exploitative and short-sighted, ignoring the realities of today. this report can ensure that the abolition of transitional periods will also remove the blockage of social rights and pave the way for a social and just europe. the crucial thing is to have a european labour market with equal pay for equal work and equal working conditions, guaranteeing equal access to social rights for all workers. at the same time, the social inspection services and transnational cooperation must be strengthened.

president, ladies and gentlemen, the acp countries are almost all at the bottom of the latest edition of the corruption perception index, a widely used measure by transparency international. that should not surprise us. what is worrying, however, is the evolution of this index in many acp countries. there's been little improvement in the last five years. this meritorious report makes it clear that eradicating corruption can significantly increase africa's gross domestic product. corruption thus makes the difference between life and death for many africans, since it primarily affects the very poor. it is therefore vital to continue the fight against corruption with adequate means rather than assume, as some illustrious figures do, that the fight against corruption is of secondary importance. it is equally important to be cautious in dealing with direct budget support. this amounts to the eu issuing blank cheques to uncontrolled foreign institutions. let us first of all help ngos with projects that can produce tangible results and for which commitments to results and quality requirements can be made.

mulder, on the general budget, and mr guidoni, on the agencies. these are the two reports in which the matters within the competence of our committee are dealt with directly. first of all, as regards the general budget of the european union, i appreciate that mr mulder has taken up in his report two of the main points made in my opinion and which were adopted by our committee. the first of these concerns the rate of budget implementation in the area of freedom, security and justice, with particular reference to payments. although this is an improvement on 2003, when it was only 68%, the with him, that many of them do not deserve this negative image and that this should be made known to the citizens of the european union by justifying, as often as necessary and by appropriate means, i would like to make three points. first of all, i would like to draw attention to the amendments i have tabled to the mulder and guidoni reports concerning the office for reconstruction in the former yugoslavia. we visited kosovo with an ad hoc delegation in february. we have seen that the office for reconstruction is doing a great job, a brilliant job, and we must not throw away this expertise. hence the need, at a crucial time, when kosovo will probably become independent this year, not to speed up the decommissioning of this office, but to gradually decommission it and also to use this expertise in other conflict regions in the world, iraq, afghanistan and other regions. the second point is the discharge of parliament. i would like to acknowledge the excellent work of my colleague, mr markus ferber. he delivered a splendid report. his draft report was even stronger than the report which was finally voted by cocobu, but we must note that there are new revelations. and that's what we need to investigate. that means that we will have to vote against his report, that we will have to vote against the discharge. i want to be very clear, ladies and gentlemen, that this does not detract from the work of mr ferber; president, ladies and gentlemen, i also wish to stress on behalf of the green group in the european parliament that if we do not grant discharge to parliament as mr ferber has asked, this will be a clear political signal that we want to win six months, that we want to have six months' delay to look at the file in depth, to look at the file of the two buildings in strasbourg to the bone and to see what has happened there. this will not be a debate on the seat, because that is not the issue. the point here is that we need to know whether our money is being well spent or whether it is being misused. that's the stake of the delay.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, tomorrow, if all goes well, parliament will give its green light to these two new regulations in the food sector. one is to clean up the jungle of foods and drinks fortified with vitamins, for example. the other aims to allow or prohibit, under certain conditions, the nutrition or health claims which increasingly accompany these same products. these are two proposals that really speak to citizens and give them concrete answers about the trust they place in these claims, to guide their purchasing choices. the beuc points out that 60% of consumers believe that a product rich in calcium can only be good for the dietary balance, which is not necessarily true. the only way to be sure of this is to know the full identity card of a food or drink, its sugar, salt, fat content. that's why we need the nutritional profile, a real nutritional profile, not a discounted one. this is what we are presented with in compromise 66, the so-called disclosure clause, which actually offers both a red light and a green light on the same package; i think that is the height of contradiction, the greatest confusion for the consumer, and i do not agree with that - it is exceptional - the analysis of our rapporteur. i shall abstain on this point, however, in order not to block this text, which is urgent. i have said on many occasions that i am opposed to any weakening of article 4 and to the authorization of certain messages extolling the virtues of alcohol. so no to amendments nos 18 and 489 as well. i would of course like to thank our two rapporteurs, mrs poli bortone and mrs karine scheel, the commission and the council, who have grasped the full importance of these citizen-oriented legislation, while taking account, as has been pointed out, of the concerns often expressed by smes, of the need also to work here in the general interest and to resist certain particular interests. i think that mrs sommer, in part of her speech, set the tone in what i found to be harsh and unjustified criticisms of the work of the commission and of our commissioner. so let me conclude that we have nothing to gain from the fact that european law is being written at the international confectionery show in cologne.
in writing. today, after a long battle, we were able to reach an agreement between parliament and the council to regulate the aforementioned claims on food products, against all expectations, from the second reading stage. i'm glad to hear it. the text we are voting for has at least two objectives: to prevent certain unfair advertising and allegations and to prevent obesity. the adoption of this text is a concrete step: it shows the european citizens, who seem to be doubting more and more the usefulness of the undertaking we are undertaking in building europe, that europe is making progress every day, that it produces concrete rules which improve their daily lives - in this case their ability to make informed choices about what they eat - in short, that it is useful! the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the labelling of products is not distorted, and i am glad that the labelling of products with a low fat content and a high sugar content will now be required to be displayed on the same side and in the same way.
in writing. i had the opportunity to express my views last night during the debate on the poli bortone report. i would like to clarify my vote on amendment no 66, the so-called disclosure clause. i am opposed to any challenge to article 4 and the nutritional profile. i therefore regard this compromise as the worst kind of hypocrisy: to allow foods high in sugar, salt or fat to be labelled as nutritious, provided that the high content of these substances is also displayed. in other words, make cheating legal! for example, "fat-free" lollipops are alive, even though they are also "pure sugar"! and so much for the cavities of children. if i abstain, despite this, it's for the rest. because, first of all, we have come a long, long way since our first reading vote that simply killed the nutritional profile. because, too, many uncertainties have been raised, particularly for smes. because the ban on health claims for alcoholic products remains in place. because i am convinced that we would not get any better at the end of a long and costly conciliation. politics is also about compromise.
in writing. this report, which is before us for our second reading vote, contains some progress in terms of health, but some of the amendments are a real step backwards. this is clearly the case with amendments nos 17, 66 and 90, which not only seek to legalise products high in fat, sugar or salt, but also allow misleading claims to be made to consumers. i very much regret the adoption of the amendments which take away a large part of the relevance of the text and regret that they have been the subject of a compromise which i do not hesitate to call dubious.
in writing. as a newly elected member of the european parliament, i made my first ever call to the european commission on a draft european law on health and nutrition claims that was being prepared. this was 14 years ago. the need for community legislation was recognized at that time. so an agreement on a concrete legal text after so many years is not too soon. the result is worth seeing. it strikes a balance between the interests and rights of consumers to health protection and information on the one hand and the need not to impose undue burdens on industry on the other. both the solution on the nutritional profiles and the notification procedure indicate that we have reached a mature approach. i therefore vote in favour of this result.

postponement, which is one of the provisions of the accession treaty. to avoid any confusion or misunderstanding, this compels me say that in fact, in the conclusion of the commission’s report adopted just three hours ago, we say that by early october we shall assess whether the two countries have been able to address the remaining shortcomings and on that basis we shall adopt a position as to whether the proposed accession date can be adhered to. this means, in clear english or other community languages, that the commission reserves the right to consider the use of this safeguard clause of postponement unless the shortcomings are addressed in the next five months, which is, as i said, fully achievable for both countries if they really make an effort. our goal is the accession of bulgaria and romania in 2007 and our duty is to see that both countries join when they meet the conditions and are fully prepared to join the european union. this is also a matter of the famous absorption capacity of the european union. that is the best way to make the enlargement a success for bulgaria, romania and europe. i trust that you will support the commission in this objective.

member of the commission. biofuels are needed for two main reasons. firstly, our energy security depends on developing alternatives to oil. among the solutions that can be put into practice today, there is none with the same potential as biofuels. secondly, the transport sector is not contributing enough to the reduction of greenhouse gas emissions. biofuels can significantly contribute to this aim. the eu has decided to achieve rapid development of biofuels use. the biofuels directive, adopted in 2003, set a reference value of a 2% market share for biofuels in 2005 and 5.75% in 2010, compared with a 0.2% share in 2000. in adopting national indicative targets under the directive, member states have been somewhat less ambitious, but, overall, the target is a share of about 1.4% in 2005. in the light of this, the review of the functioning of the biofuels directive, which the commission is due to carry out this year, is particularly important. we have just launched the public consultation exercise for this review. the public consultation poses a number of questions. first of all, will the 5.75% objective for 2010 be achieved through the present policies and measures? if not, what can be done to ensure that the objective will be achieved? should the community set objectives for the share of biofuels in 2015 and 2020? the commission has taken no position on these questions so far. turning to the question of environmental impact, it is important to start from the basis that biofuels bring a number of environmental benefits. however, it is also true that their production can have some adverse effects on the environment. i believe that second-generation biofuels can have even greater environmental benefits and their introduction should be accelerated as much as possible. the commission will make sure, therefore, that the promotion of biofuels continues to bring benefits in environmental terms, as well as the benefit of security of supply. for that reason, as part of the review of the directive, the commission is asking for views on the introduction of a certification system. this could ensure that only biofuels whose cultivation meets required environmental standards count towards objectives in the directive. the initial reaction of environmental ngos, fuel suppliers and other stakeholders has been encouragingly positive. as regards car manufacturers' obligations, carmakers have voluntarily agreed to limit average co2 emissions from new cars to 140g per km by 2008/2009. the commission does not consider that the use of biofuels should in any way reduce the objectives agreed upon with the car industry.

president. - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, over the past decade the european parliament has played a decisive role in the political response to mad cow disease. this disease broke out in the united kingdom in the 1980s, but took on a new dimension when the british minister for health told the british parliament in westminster that there was probably a link between mad cow disease and a new variant of creutzfeldt-jakob disease. the political response from parliament was swift: the commission has also been asked to draw up a report on the situation in the community, which is to be submitted to the council. a conditional motion of censure was introduced and one of the main results was that food safety was removed from the remit of dg agriculture and became one of the policy areas covered by the codecision procedure. however, since the adoption of the bse legislation in 2001 and 2002 and the detailed rules on animal by-products, a number of controversial measures have also been adopted under the comitology procedure. the bse legislation has been updated by comitology decisions on no less than 19 occasions since june 2001. the european parliament must therefore be vigilant. just two examples. the commission's proposal to re-feed fish meal to ruminants was only withdrawn after strong protests in this parliament. second example: the provisions on feeding described in annex 4 to the act were changed by comitology decisions without the involvement of the european parliament from detailed descriptions of half a page to no less than 9 pages of detailed provisions and derogations. the comitology procedure therefore remains a huge problem. we have not been able to resolve this in this round of negotiations with the council. we are pleased, and it is certainly positive, that scientific risk analysis of the effects on humans and animals should also be taken into account in future. the agreement reached with the council at first reading, which is reflected in amendments nos 41 to 56, has the full support of our group. our main points remain: the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's fisheries policy is not distorted by the introduction of new species of fish, such as the ban on feeding all animal proteins to ruminants, the provision that fishmeal can only be fed to young animals of ruminant species after a scientific assessment and sufficiently strong control measures, and the rules on separator meat, which must now be finally agreed. i am particularly sorry that we cannot have this debate in the presence of the rapporteur, mr dagmar roth-behrendt. i wish her much strength, much health and hope that she can return to her role in this parliament very soon.
the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competent authority' to cover the whole of the european union. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, as rapporteur for the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs, i should like, after thanking mr böge and our negotiators, to sum up the specific reasons which have led our committee to endorse, without feeling, the draft interinstitutional agreement which has been presented to us. the libe committee made four specific requests at the outset of the negotiation process. firstly, we have asked for the freedom, security and justice sector to be the subject of a specific and autonomous sub-heading in the new financial framework in order to underline its political importance. we have obtained this subheading: it is subheading 3a. secondly, we have proposed a substantial increase in appropriations to anticipate the threat of terrorism and the development of organised crime, and to meet the requirements of better external border control and better management of migration flows. we have obtained this increase in appropriations. over the next seven years, the appropriations allocated to the creation of the area of freedom, security and justice could increase by 167%. this is by far the highest potential growth in the new financial framework. thirdly, we asked for permission to finance the new agencies outside the financial framework, in order to preserve the resources allocated to the operational programmes. if i understand the provision in part iii, point d correctly, in future it will be necessary to obtain the agreement of both arms of the budgetary authority to finance any new agency before it is set up. this financing will not therefore be automatically covered by the financial perspective. we can be satisfied with that, even if we had hoped for better. finally, we have proposed that a sort of trilogue be established with parliament and the council in the libe sector before the council's first reading on the budget, in order to harmonize the positions of the two institutions on political priorities and the allocation of resources. it is true that this provision is not mentioned as such in the text of the interinstitutional agreement, but it is a unilateral declaration by the european parliament: it is declaration 13. we have not got everything we asked for, but it is true that this is a little better and, in the light of the times, mr president, ladies and gentlemen, a little better - to use the words of raymond devos - is not nothing, it is already something.
the european parliament, thanks to the negotiating skills of reimer böge and the other negotiators, has succeeded in giving the original council proposals real european added value and in significantly improving the institutional framework, but the financial perspective for 2007-2013 remains insufficient in the light of the challenge facing the european union. the european parliament, however, had put forward a reasonable and reasoned proposal in the böge report, which was more realistic than the commission's proposal. the council's narrow-mindedness and short-sightedness on the basis of national considerations, however, have outstripped the european ambition of parliament and the commission. we shall nevertheless approve this interinstitutional agreement. it is the only compromise possible under the present circumstances. the lack of financial prospects for the coming years would plunge the union into an even deeper crisis than it is already in. it would also be wrong to do so in the case of the new member states, which need a long-term perspective to continue their cohesion policy and integrate fully into the single market. this does not mean that the current level of resources is insufficient to meet the new european challenges. the revision clause and the flexibility scheme were therefore absolutely necessary. the risk is that sooner or later we will be stuck. parliament must also be fully involved in these. the most important conclusion, however, is that the current system of own resources has reached its limits. there is a need for a fundamental reform. we must get rid of the system of subsidies from national budgets. we must return to genuine own resources, as was the case with customs duties at the start of european integration. this is the only way to put the european general interest ahead of the narrow and short-sighted national interest. the european parliament must play a role - a driving role - and make proposals. the studies and notes of mr lamassoure can provide a good starting point. the european parliament, mr president, has managed to save an extra 4 billion euros in the showdown with the council. we are talking about a total of €864 billion over the period 2007 to 2013. the council also de facto gave parliament more powers by allowing it to evaluate the structure of the budget in 2008-09. we are still in the so-called reflection period on the future of the european union. a reflection period can and should also provide an opportunity to question a number of issues. we are now, for example, moving towards a european tax in the form of a tax on energy and transport. in the name of the common interest of the european taxpayers we must oppose this new tax, a new tax which, however it is turned, will add to the fiscal pressure which is already too high in most member states.
in writing. i abstained because i cannot accept that the research budget in the next three years will not be increased in real terms, whereas the lisbon process, the statements of the heads of state, rightly consider this to be a key priority for europe.
in writing. i was not very enthusiastic about approving the financial perspective and the interinstitutional agreement. it is a very low ambition budget, but without this agreement we cannot offer the new member states any prospects at all. in addition, many of the programmes are in danger of being shut down. a new crisis by rejecting the financial perspective would cause enormous damage to the european union.
in writing. like any compromise, this one has some weaknesses. but it is a step forward and it is worth supporting. accession negotiations, new initiatives, including in research, required more resources and it would have been a bad sign for europe, for the new member states and for the legitimate expectations of solidarity between the regions of europe if the countries had not agreed to this compromise. however, it is also clear that such negotiations need to be more open and transparent. it is not acceptable that certain countries should demand solidarity in one area - agriculture, for example - and refuse it in other areas - the structural funds, for example. this europe of 'egoism' must be brought to an end, slowly, in the interests of all.
in writing. final step on the financial perspective (period 2007-2013) with the adoption of an overall envelope of €866.4 billion. one figure: europe costs each european 26 cents a day. after the blockades by some member states (see the "club of the six") who wanted a discounted deal, the compromise reached can be welcomed, although it is far below the initial proposal of the parliament of june 2005, namely 975 billion euros. parliament has nevertheless managed to extract an additional 4 billion from the council's decision, 4 billion allocated mainly to education policies (40 000 additional erasmus grants!) research, competitiveness and innovation. on the other hand, other areas are being sacrificed, such as 'life +', a major environmental programme which will only be financed at 16% of the amount originally planned. in conclusion, a compromise agreement, a budget for survival, which ensures a certain continuity, but which is far from giving europe the means to achieve its ambitions.
in writing. i did not approve the interinstitutional agreement. the ep asked for €112 billion more than the council for the period 2007-13. the leaders of the major political groups shouted at blair's debriefing in december 2005 that he was a murderer and said that they would not accept this result under any circumstances. five months later, we find that the pressure from the capitals and especially from berlin has been very effective: all the expensive promises were swallowed. the ep is satisfied with an increase of just under €4 billion. this agreement will fail to adequately address the major challenges facing the eu. the second pillar of the agricultural policy, which is to finance rural development and focus on quality, animal welfare and environmental protection, is being curtailed. but also the trans-european networks, programmes such as erasmus (only 140,000 students will now be sent instead of 170,000 in 2006!!!) and leonardo (36,000 beneficiaries instead of 50,000!!!), the seventh r & d programme, the social agenda, the structural funds, life+ and natura, cultural activities, foreign policy and our actions towards our neighbours are all under-funded. the expectations of the people of europe will not be met. and that is a great pity.
in writing. between the parliament's vote in june 2005 (€974 billion) and the agreement under the uk presidency in december 2005 (€862 billion), there was a €112 billion difference. after many meetings, the trialogue agreement has made it possible to raise an additional 2 billion; which is far from satisfactory. but at a time when euroscepticism is gaining ground in the old and new member states, particularly because of the uncertainties linked to enlargement and the institutional situation, i think it is useful to reassure by adopting the financial perspectives for seven years. solidarity mechanisms are maintained if they do not grow in importance as i would have wished. for these reasons i am voting for the financial perspective, but of course without enthusiasm.
in writing. i voted in favour of the interinstitutional agreement on budgetary discipline and sound financial management. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. i think that parliament has done its job well, better than ever and above all better than the other two institutions. the fact that we have serious prospects despite the european commission's lack of commitment and the greed of governments is due to our preparation and our negotiators. in the present circumstances, this agreement is better than absolute uncertainty about the financial policy space. there 's one thing that 's been bothering me for a while . the commission has already taken a number of initiatives in this field, including the introduction of a new system of agricultural prices, the introduction of a new system of agricultural prices, the introduction of a new system of agricultural prices, the introduction of a new system of agricultural prices, the introduction of a new system of agricultural prices, the introduction of a new system of agricultural prices. but since we have no guarantee that we can pursue a more positive agricultural policy - without the constitution - and without financial prospects, this was not a sufficient reason to reject the whole thing.
in writing. in june 2005, parliament estimated that the union needed a budget of €974 billion to meet the challenges of tomorrow. the agreement on the financial perspective reached on 4 april at the end of the trilogue between parliament, the commission and the council is €112 billion below our position in june 2005. we have seen a 35% reduction in budgetary resources for competitiveness and growth (heading 1a), a 10% reduction for the structural and cohesion funds (heading 1b), etc. with such a tight budget, it is hard to imagine that the union can meet the objectives of lisbon and gothenburg. it is a minimalist agreement which does not give the union sufficient room for manoeuvre to pursue new policies for the future. the only merit of these financial perspectives is that they exist! we cannot postpone our vote for fear of not being able to start the new programme on time. in particular, member states and regions must be able to prepare and submit their projects in time for them to be co-financed as from 2007. it would be tragic for europe's poorest regions if they were not able to receive structural aid as early as 2007. i shall abstain for the following reasons.
in writing. the programme is very valuable in terms of 'greener' mobility. however, if we want to relieve the road, this must be done with great efficiency of the other modes of transport (water rail). however, multimodal transport is as strong as each link in the chain. however, rail remains the weak partner and a recent survey of multimodal platforms clearly shows where the weak points are. it would therefore be advisable for the commission and parliament to deal directly with this issue beyond the present programmes, otherwise the best support programmes will be based on the inefficiency of rail transport, among others, and transport companies will not see any benefits in using the railways.
the commission's proposal is to be submitted to the council for adoption. it is a call for greater coordination and coherence in eu and member states' human rights policies. we all know that the rapporteur dreams of a single common human rights report reflecting the views of the council, the commission and parliament. this dream can and will become a reality if the council takes parliament's opinion seriously in the short term and subordinates economic and commercial considerations to the principle of the universality and indivisibility of human rights in the long term. i agree with the rapporteur that the european initiative for democracy and human rights is a very important instrument in the european union's human rights strategy. i also share the view of commissioner ferrero-waldner that more resources should be allocated to election observation missions, which are crucial in strengthening democracy and human rights, especially in post-conflict situations. i also expect a lot from the inclusion of human rights clauses in all eu agreements. it is one thing to set up good instruments, but it is quite another to apply them consistently and courageously. it is therefore incomprehensible that the commission continues to hesitate to apply article 96 of the cotonou agreement in certain cases of flagrant violation of human rights in acp countries, as in ethiopia for example. a policy of double standards threatens to undermine the credibility of eu human rights policy and must be avoided at all costs. the merit of the howitt report is that it goes beyond the casuistry and finally offers the prospect of a more systematic and integrated approach. in that sense, i support it with great enthusiasm.
mr. president, the most important news for me tonight is that barça won. i'm very happy for you. mr president, as a member of the alde group and as rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs for the 2007 budget, i would first of all like to note that, among the limited number of political priorities which have been selected by our general rapporteur, mr elles, for the 2007 budget, the security dimension of the european union, both internal and external, is in a good place. no one can deny that the prevention and fight against terrorism, the improvement of external border controls and the dismantling of clandestine immigration channels are real and serious problems which require appropriate policies and adequate resources. nor can anyone deny that these are areas in which integrated action at european level brings an undeniable added value compared with the efforts of member states acting in isolation. and finally, no one can deny that these are problems which concern the citizens of the union very strongly and very legitimately, as all the surveys and all the barometers show, and which the european citizens consider not only normal but also necessary to be dealt with at european level. i believe, mr president, madam commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, that to reconcile europe and its citizens, we must probably have much less fumeous discussions and much less propaganda pamphlets, but much more policies and measures which respond effectively to the problems which people are really experiencing and which they feel are important and priorities. homeland security is one of these domains. the 2007 budget will give us the opportunity to begin to stop disappointing them.
mr president, in september 2003, following the unprecedented forest fires that had struck portugal and a whole series of regions in southern europe, we took the floor to urge the european union to set up a permanent rapid reaction force. we were then, and still are, calling in the four excellent reports before us for, i would say, a little less national selfishness in civil protection, but also, as mrs estrella proposes in paragraphs 3 and 4 of her report, for an intensification of european cooperation in civil protection. mr janusz wojciechowski has spoken at length on this point. intensification of european cooperation for at least two good reasons. firstly, the impact of climate change on the frequency of natural disasters, the torrential rains in northern and eastern europe, the drought, the fires last year are here to remind us of the urgent need to act and to act quickly in this matter. secondly, the financial cost of these disasters is increasing and our member states are finding it increasingly difficult to bear it alone. the purpose of the solidarity fund is to cover these emergency costs, such as the supply of water, energy, housing and repairs to basic infrastructure. i should like to take this opportunity to thank mr berend, who wants to facilitate immediate access to part of the payments and who also proposes to extend the benefit of the fund to other types of disasters, such as drought or - he has spoken of this - threats or terrorist attacks of a biological or chemical nature. the european union will of course never be able to prevent all natural disasters, but it is our duty to assist the europeans who are struck by these tragedies and to coordinate our efforts better in these circumstances. that is why, despite the reluctance of some member states, we must also support the creation of a permanent european civil protection force, the famous 'green helmets'. we also support the less controversial creation of a major crisis management operational centre. you see, commissioner piebalgs, and i say this in response to a fear expressed by mrs haug, which i share, that we are not asking for more europe, but for a better europe.

the presence and speech of the belgian prime minister, mr verhofstadt, in this debate, mr president, can be considered a good illustration of what is fundamentally wrong with this european union. the fundamental problem with europe and verhofstadt is that the so-called statesmen no longer have even the most elementary democratic reflex, are no longer prepared to listen to the people and to the voters, and are convinced that they know better from their ivory towers. we saw another example of that just a few minutes ago. now prime minister verhofstadt is calling on his european colleagues to ignore the devastating referendums in france and the netherlands and just continue to pretend that nothing is going on. in his own country, verhofstadt did not even dare to take part in the promised referendum, although he is very keen to be seen as the champion of participation in referendums. but mr verhofstadt's participation should only be organised if he is sure of being proved right, not if he is in danger of losing. the most obvious example of the european democratic deficit is the case of turkey, and it is the case of turkey that threatens to paralyse the whole european system. europe is not stuck because of the expressed and justified distrust of voters in the netherlands and france, but because of the reason for that distrust. europe is stuck because high-ranking european statesmen refuse to define the territory of the european union, refuse to limit the potential accession of new candidate countries to countries that are culturally, geographically, historically also part of europe, which is obviously not the case for turkey. verhofstadt herself is one of the most outspoken defenders of turkish accession and refuses to allow any referendum or any democratic participation in this area. a month ago, the other ivory tower wise guy, european commission president barroso, said that he saw no reason to debate european borders, because such a debate would lead nowhere. well, the opposite is true. only when the citizens of europe are reassured that europe will have borders, only when they know that their europe will be limited to countries with a common base essential to them, only when european citizens feel that their statesmen are once again willing to listen to their voice and are willing to respect the outcome of democratic consultation, only then and not before can the european recovery begin. once again, it was good and symbolic in that sense that it was the belgian prime minister verhofstadt, the man who in his own country represents only 15% of the votes, who came here to represent the europe of the high-level, know-it-all and never-listening eurocrats, because that performance was tailor-made for him.
i would like to welcome this debate, mr president, which i hope will be the first in a series, because i believe that parliament can indeed contribute to reviving the momentum in debates with heads of government. it is good that prime minister verhofstadt was here to introduce this because he is indeed one of those who has made an active contribution during the period of reflection. i can agree with his approach to double-tracking to a large extent. we must move forward with ratification, but at the same time we must not sit back and take initiatives. i am less in agreement with him when he says that we can continue to expand unchecked in the meantime. i am not against enlargement, but further enlargement without deepening the european union seems to me to lead to what we do not want, a free trade area. what i do think is that we must make an even greater effort to make it clear to our citizens that what they fear at the moment, namely change, globalisation, globalisation, is not a retreat in itself into some form of economic patriotism or nationalism, but that only a european response is a real response to globalisation; the european union must also adapt to the new realities of globalisation and the information society, and we can only do this together with europe. because only together with europe are we able to be a true global actor defending our interests, our values, also in the global world of tomorrow. at the moment our citizens are not aware of this. they still think that by closing ourselves together, we can save ourselves. we must teach them that globalisation is irreversible. that will happen without europe if we do not stand strong. we can play a very strong role in globalisation if europe is strong.

the rapporteur. mr president, commissioner, this report on women in conflict and reconstruction is based on three key documents. first, un resolution 1325, the report by maj britt theorin on the same issue in 2000 in our chamber and finally the disturbing report by elisabeth rehn and ellen johnson sirleaf, now president of liberia, also on this subject. since these reports, what's been going on? well, today, in 2006, there is still a lot to be done, and my report wanted to shed light on three aspects. the first is that of women as victims of war. i would like to say that the situation in this area, which has already been discussed at length, is unsustainable. today women are still raped, but they are raped because rape is a weapon of war. even today, vaginas are still being gutted with bayonets. even today, my dear colleagues, we take children alive from their mother's womb. today, in the camps of displaced persons, the sexual exploitation of women and children is not allowed but is common, including by peacekeeping forces, including members of the diplomatic corps. this is untenable! the second aspect i wanted to mention is that of women as peacemakers. because all over the world, in these chaotic situations, there are brave women who are calling for justice, who are reaching out to the belligerents and saying, we women want peace. and these associative structures, we must protect them, we must push them and we must allow these women of peace, once at the negotiating table, to have an equal share and to have a decisive role in the construction of their democracies. we owe them that much. the third aspect is even more delicate, because women are not only carriers of peace, they are also - alas - carriers of war, and the american soldier in abu ghraib prison is a terrible example of this. so women can be cruel, and i wanted to look at an area that is extremely sensitive, and that is the female suicide bomber. women suicide bombers are not a new phenomenon, but it is a phenomenon that is currently growing. in chechnya, 50% of women are suicide bombers, in palestine we have seen a wave of female suicide bombers. what is the cause? we have to analyse it in parliament, we have to do a study, we have to do a conference, but we already know that there are three factors involved. first, it is true, a distorted reading of islam, this great religion. because it's in fundamentalist countries that this happens. the second aspect is that of resistance in situations of war which violate international law every day, a resistance which we must understand without admitting it and condemning the innocent victims it kills. the third factor is linked to societies where women are culturally marginalized, where sometimes they are excluded from their villages because they have been raped. that's where we're going to look for female suicide bombers, among women who have already been victims of society. i therefore ask the commission and parliament to look into this problem. what can be done today in relation to all this? but there's still a lot to do! everything has been said, all the measures have been defined, but what we have to do is to apply them. implementing better reproductive health services is true, but that is not the main theme of this report. in all our financial instruments, in all our programmes - the stability instrument, the neighbourhood policy - and in our action plans, we must apply measures which take account of the gender issue and the dignity of women. we must also ensure that our police forces are trained, because we now have them, to respect women's rights, to be sensitive to them and to include women in their ranks. in this huge construction project, there is both hope - these are the women of peace - and concern. we have a lot of work to do and i thank all my colleagues on the committee on women's rights who have been able to go beyond the ideological debate and say 'we agree with this report'. i will respect everything that has been voted in the committee on women's rights and i would also like to thank the chairman of the committee on women's rights, mrs záborská, who is not here today but who sent me a note this morning. it must be said: we women will overcome partisan politics in this area. thank you in advance for your support. in writing. with the adoption of this recommendation on medicinal products for paediatric use and the agreement between parliament and the council, children will now be able to benefit from medicines adapted to their specific metabolism rather than being prescribed low doses of medicines intended for adults. it seems to me that we have tried here to meet all the conditions necessary for the intelligent development of specific pharmaceutical forms for children in europe: provisions to support innovation and research, incentives for laboratories (including the additional six months of protection of the certificate of protection), the creation of an inventory of specific needs in paediatrics, the obligation to develop a paediatric form for new medicinal products, measures to ensure that these products, once developed, are distributed throughout the member states, or exceptional derogations provided for so that the development of paediatric products does not, however, impede the provision of medicinal products for adults. this is a concrete example of the added value of community regulation: an isolated member state would never have had the means to promote such a policy on its own in respect of these specific medicines.

in writing. i strongly support the initiative of the kingdom of belgium, which is essential for effective cooperation between member states in the protection of children against sexual abuse. the least that can be said is that this initiative fills a gap, a void, what can i say! the current abyss. let us realize - and belgium saw the gravity of this when the appalling fourniret case was discovered - that at present, when a person is convicted in a member state of paedophilia and is prohibited in that state from engaging in activities which might put him in contact with children, all he has to do is to escape that prohibition by moving to another member state! at present, therefore, there is no guarantee that a revocation pronounced in one member state will have legal effect in the other member states. in the face of this odious situation, it was time to put in place a system which obliges the state of residence of the person convicted of sexual offences against children to recognise the prohibitions issued abroad and to apply them on its own territory. i am convinced that this text will save children from the horror of the holocaust.
