the commission has already taken a number of initiatives to improve the quality of the environment, and has recently launched a new environmental action programme. in order to involve citizens more closely in the implementation of environmental policy at all levels of government, better access to spatial environmental information is of great importance. with inspire, as you know, we want to set up an infrastructure for spatial information in europe, one of the main objectives of which is the exchange and sharing of data between public authorities. it is also intended to be the engine for the collection of new relevant environmental information and, ultimately, to be a tool for making environmental policy more efficient and effective across borders. the existing data infrastructures and environmental information in the member states will be better coordinated and made readable at eu level. the proposal covers both information needed to monitor the state of the environment and information to improve it. i think that all policy decisions which have a direct or indirect impact on the environment can be taken into account. i would like to give some examples. in order to protect citizens from flooding - and mr seeber, as rapporteur, will confirm this in due course - standardization and the exchange of data on river basins, watercourses and risks are of inestimable value. we will also be able to monitor and predict forest fires, such as we have seen in southern europe, but for this we need data on soil types, vegetation, humidity and wind direction. but data on the migration of schools, the water temperature and pollution also help to strike a better balance between the needs of fisheries on the one hand and the survival of certain fish species on the other. finally, in the context of avian influenza, for example, as we are experiencing it or hopefully not yet experiencing it, joint action could be even more efficient if data on poultry farms, wildlife and migratory birds across borders were available. the added value is also great for a number of other major european projects, such as gmis, as inspire can be the supplier of data needed to set up an operational global information system for environmental and safety monitoring within gmis. but enough about the importance and added value of inspire for those who may still have doubts about this. i would like to say a few words about the procedure, because about a year ago we almost unanimously adopted the report at first reading. the council reached a political agreement at the end of the luxembourg presidency and the common position followed in january this year. in the second reading report, as we are discussing it today, the committee on the environment has again emphasized some elements which we consider very important but which were deleted in the common position. let me just list the most important ones. we believe that public consultation and search services are very important and should be free of charge. free downloads are out of the question. you can, but you don't have to. the ability to embed click permissions remains, and for clarity: the sale of maps and atlases, for example, is not affected. parliament also opposes the use of unlimited analyses and additional feasibility studies in order to avoid the inspire project being drowned in advance. the committee on environmental management also considers that intellectual property rights of public authorities cannot be an argument for refusing access to environmental data. the exchange of data between public authorities does not exclude fees or authorisations. the proposal even provides that the financial viability of public authorities should be a concern of the member states. i therefore share the regret of my fellow shadow rapporteurs, whom i would like to thank very warmly for their very constructive cooperation on this very difficult and very technical dossier, that we were unable to reach an agreement with the council at second reading. however, an open and honest dialogue in the public interest must be possible, even during a conciliation procedure, and i think this report provides a good basis for that. i thank the commission and all the members for their support.
on behalf of the pse group. mr president, commissioner, i would first of all like to thank mr buzek for the excellent cooperation and the spirit which has guided the preparation of this framework programme and, like him, i would say that the compromise amendments are amendments which are followed by all the groups and which therefore indicate the desire of parliament to clarify certain points. as regards the european research council, commissioner, i would like to say that it is clear that this is a new institution which is essential for the scientific community. we are therefore, like you, very concerned that it should function as well as possible within the framework of the autonomy of the scientific community. that is the meaning we give to our amendments on this subject, although legally it will have to be discussed during the trilogue. however, we would like to assure you of our interest in the european research council and the importance we attach to it. as regards the framework programme in general, we are, like you, a little disappointed by the reduction in resources because we know how important it is as an instrument for the lisbon policy. we also know that it can have a tremendous multiplier effect. in this respect, technology platforms and joint technology initiatives will play a key role, and it will not necessarily be a question of money, but rather of establishing, at european level, an effective public/private partnership on a few major themes. however, all these programmes must be based on excellence. this is a key factor in selecting projects, whether for collaborative research or for the 'capacities', 'ideas' and, above all, 'staff' strands, i.e. researchers, because what matters is having researchers who feel good about themselves. but today in europe researchers are not always given the social and moral status they deserve. that is why the european union must set an example by awarding quality marie curie grants, which build bridges between countries and help to slow down the brain drain. as regards the question of embryonic stem cells, which you raised, commissioner, it is true that this research component represents a very small part of the budget, barely one-thousandth. like you, i believe that the projects carried out under the sixth framework programme give every assurance of ethical quality and of the will to create projects at european level that are conducive to excellence. that is why the compromise amendment adopted in the committee on industry, research and energy on the initiative of mrs gutiérrez, which i co-signed and which you support, calls for continuing what has been done with the sixth framework programme, namely a case-by-case analysis which is essential to achieving a level of excellence in a promising research sector for the future. as far as the budget is concerned, as we said, we are all a little disappointed. i will personally see to it that in the first few years you do try to increase the relative share a little. it is true that an increase is expected, but it will be particularly marked after 2009. i therefore think that for the 2007 and 2008 budgets you should be able, within the commission, on the basis of the very good implementation rates, to increase the relative share, at least the share that parliament obtained at the end of the discussion on the financial perspective. although this share is somewhat symbolic, since it is only 340 million euros, we want it to be allocated to priorities such as the european research council and the marie curie fellowships, not to mention the thematic priorities. parliament is very concerned about the environment and energy and so these are the thematic priorities we want to support more. i would also like to draw your attention - i think you are, but, to my surprise, the council is less attentive - to the interaction between science and society. it is essential that we in europe develop a positive relationship between society and science, that we ensure that young people retain a taste for science and studies. the science and society strand is an example in this respect throughout europe and we will therefore be very careful not to cut its budget as the council has proposed. finally, i would also like to say, as others have done, that the jrc plays a vital role and that its activities in the nuclear sector contribute to harmonizing the safety issues we all need at european level. finally, rapid progress will be needed on the rules for participation. simplification of the programmes is a key point for the best possible participation. in this respect, we will have to keep a close eye on the timetable, because, as you have pointed out, 1 january 2007 is fast approaching. parliament will make a positive contribution to finding solutions to the rules of participation, as it is doing with regard to the seventh framework programme which we are about to vote on. that said, it is also a matter of the other two partners, the commission - and i have no doubt of your good will, commissioner - and the council taking matters into their own hands so that the scientific community can have member of the commission. mr president, i would first of all like to congratulate the rapporteur on the very good and constructive work he has done on this report, as of course do the members of the committee who have worked with him. i am pleased to see that the european parliament and the european commission are taking the same approach to such a serious matter. of course, i should stress at the outset that we are talking about the possibility of a pandemic; a potential pandemic. it's not a certainty! it could be caused by the avian flu virus, it could be caused by some other virus, it could be nothing at all. but the fact that it is a possibility, even though our scientists are telling us that it is a very high possibility, gives us no excuse not to prepare adequately, sufficiently, appropriately and as long as possible. it may be the first time in human history that we have a warning, that we have some signals that a pandemic like this is likely to come. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. i am particularly pleased about the report because most of what it asks us to do we have either already achieved or are in the process of covering and achieving. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. i would like to mention the example of the solidarity fund, which will help in the event of a pandemic to meet the pharmaceutical needs for vaccines and antivirals. it is a little over a year since we first discussed this matter in the house and i can say that we have achieved a great deal since then. we have now succeeded in having national preparedness and readiness plans in all the member states, we have the community readiness plan - the one we are discussing today - we have tested all the plans in a readiness exercise last november and we are now in the process of improving these plans based on the weaknesses identified in that exercise. there's very close cooperation with the world health organization. the european centre for disease control (ecdc) has taken the lead in this area and will soon be taking over the early warning system. i agree with the need for communication and information as far as citizens are concerned, and we have already started work in this area, we have organised meetings of the responsible representatives of the ministries of health of the member states and together with all the relevant organisations on how to handle messages to the public in a crisis and now we are proceeding with seminars and workshops with the media themselves to inform them, to inform them and to develop a partnership that will be exploited in a crisis. we're moving on to the issue of creating a pandemic vaccine. the european medicines agency is working in this area, we are working with industry, we are investing in research, we are trying to speed up procedures and of course to increase the production capacity of the pharmaceutical industry. as regards antivirals, we have already achieved a doubling of the production capacity of antiviral medicines compared to the previous year, but now the challenge is for the member states to exploit this capacity. it is their responsibility to make the necessary orders. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect, and i am sure that the commission will be able to support them. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect, and i am sure that the commission will be able to support them. but this is a bit misleading, because it is not the same in every member state. some member states have more than 25% coverage and others almost none. it is therefore of particular importance that we set the necessary targets and cover these countries with anti-viral medicines. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full, and i would like to ask the commission to take the necessary steps to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. the commission has not proposed this, it is a matter for the member states. the commission has made no attempt to extend and increase its powers and responsibilities, but we have adopted this recommendation because we see the usefulness of having a european strategic reserve on the model of the reserve held by the world health organisation. it would cover member states until they have their national stocks and could also cover neighbouring countries and be used strategically in the first cases of pandemic firefighting to enable member states to cope with a prolonged pandemic. there is no legal basis for this at community level. it should have been done by unanimous agreement of the member states, which should have financed this effort. unfortunately, in the debate at the last council it appeared that a very small number of member states were opposed in principle to the creation of this strategic reserve. the overwhelming majority supported the case in principle, but a very large number of member states were not willing to meet these needs with national resources. i believe that in view of these positions of the member states there is no room for further discussion or further work on this subject. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. the continuation of an already lengthy debate, without any prospect of agreement on the horizon, simply sends the wrong message, gives false hope, especially to the citizens and to the member states which are currently in need of this reserve, so it is better that everyone knows where he stands and therefore that each member state takes up its responsibilities. as i said at the beginning, most of the measures to be taken for pandemic preparedness are primarily the responsibility of the member states, but the european commission will continue to coordinate and take the initiative, to have a leading role in this preparation member of the commission. mr president, i should like to thank the members for a very interesting debate. once again it has been confirmed that we have the same approach and that we see eye to eye on this issue. almost all aspects of the preparedness plans are generic in nature. that means that, when we prepare for a possible flu pandemic, we are preparing not just for the possibility of avian flu, but for any flu pandemic and, when it comes to it, for any health threat. since they are mostly generic in nature, they can be used in any kind of health threat and they are therefore a good investment on the part of the member states and the community that will not go to waste. we should all pray that they do go to waste, that we do not have a health crisis and will never actually need to use them, but nevertheless it is a good investment in preparedness, both for natural pandemics and for bioterrorism. we are much better prepared than we were last year or the year before. we have not yet reached the target and we can never actually say that we have. it is a continuous effort and there is always room for improvement, always a need to do more. nevertheless, we have really come a long way, but we still have much to do. we still do not know a great deal about the specific virus. that is why we have invested so much in research into the specific avian flu virus and how it behaves. we have certain statistics, but given the areas where the virus usually occurs and is endemic, the reporting and monitoring is not as effective as in some other areas. we still lack data and will therefore be investing in this. i will not go into the animal health side, since we had an opportunity to discuss that recently in this house. however, the basic policy of the european union, as well as the world community, is to make sure that avian flu remains an animal disease. in order to do that, we have to eradicate and deal with it effectively at the animal level. cooperation is very important. we had the post-beijing conference in vienna last week, where practical steps were discussed for implementing and fulfilling the pledges already made in beijing. it is true that progress was slow. i am optimistic that things will speed up now. all the european commission decisions required for the eur 100 million pledged are in place now and it will start being spent on the various initiatives. the next meeting will be in africa and will cover the problems of the region. i must remind you that the european community has pledged money specifically for the african continent. i agree that it is very important to have the involvement of all services. that is what we have done in our specific plan and that is what we encourage member states to do in their own national plans. there should be a lead preparedness and response authority, but all services in each member state and at community level as well should be involved. the interoperability of national plans is also very important and we have been working on that. i totally agree with those of you who raised the issue, for example, of borders closing. in the reality of the european union, one member state cannot close its borders or cancel flights from a specific place when we have the internal market and freedom of movement. we need cooperation and coordination. we have already set up the structures and mechanisms to achieve that at community level. we have raised this issue at g8 level – international level – as well, proposing the ‘no first use’ principle, so that no g8 partner would close its borders and cancel flights without at least consulting the others in the group. i take your point on the issue of the institutions. as you know, we have been working on continuity of commission services. we have also asked parliament to look into this matter, and will be working closely with parliament’s services to ensure continuity within parliament and its meetings and services. antivirals are certainly a debated issue. antivirals are not a panacea, they are not a cure, they are the first line of defence and we have always said that. but as long as we do not have the pandemic virus, we will not have the pandemic vaccine which would be the basic defence. we are trying to shorten the period required to develop an approved licence and produce the vaccine, but we will need something in the meantime and antivirals are one solution. as regards the issue of stockpiles, it is not just that some member states object to the principle, but there is also reluctance by many member states on the funding of the stockpile. we explained that it will not be a substitute for national stockpiles. on the contrary, in our options paper we have introduced a condition that, in order to be able to benefit, a member state will have to prove that it has actually begun creating its own national stockpile. that would be a great relief, not only for the member states that are still in the process of developing stockpiles, but for the neighbouring countries as well, and for the who. the who would not then have to worry about the european region, because we would take care of ourselves and our neighbours and the who could concentrate on other regions in greater need than us. i totally agree with mrs grossetête, but i am afraid that was not the response we received. i was disappointed, but at the same time we have to be realistic. i do not want this debate to continue and then be used as an excuse for some member states not to develop national stockpiles. unless the council changes the position, the austrian presidency, ‘challenges’ – if i can use the expression – the member states to say they will be willing to review the position. it is important to emphasise that member states will have to create their own national stockpiles. i totally agree that this is a question of solidarity. to be realistic, if we cannot agree on the solidarity principle now, what is going to happen if there is a crisis and one member state has to share its medicine with other member states, with all the citizens feeling threatened? one of the weaknesses detected at the exercise last november was the unwillingness on the part of member states to share medicine. we therefore need to establish solidarity now, when we can be more realistic in our approach than in a time of crisis. then it can work in the way we would all wish. nevertheless, we will try to coordinate the member states and at least make sure that they take all the necessary steps for the sake of their individual citizens and european citizens in general. i will, as always, keep parliament informed of every step we take.

member of the commission. in response to the honourable member’s question on an alleged infringement of industrial property rights, the commission stresses that it cannot intervene in a legal dispute between private parties pending before the court of a member state. if a party believes that its industrial property rights have been infringed and sues in a national court, it is for the national judge to ascertain whether an infringement has taken place. it is also for the national judge to consider whether the relevant national legislation is in line with european community law and with international treaties to which the relevant country is a party. european community law governing the enforcement of intellectual property rights applies to trade names only insofar as these are protected as exclusive property rights in the national law concerned. in any event, the european community is not a contracting party to the paris convention on the protection of industrial property. the honourable member should also note that article 15.1 of the electronic commerce directive prohibits the application of a general filtering obligation to internet hosting services. according to article 14.3 of that directive, it is only a court or an administrative authority that can require a given service provider to terminate or prevent a specific infringing activity. in addition, the commission does not consider that the points raised by the honourable member relate to the market power or the potential monopoly that the company in question may have.

member of the commission. article 152 makes it clear that the organisation and delivery of health services and medical care are the responsibility of member states, as i mentioned before. competent national authorities are free to make their own decisions on the pricing and reimbursement of pharmaceutical articles, provided that these decisions are made in a transparent manner and do not create barriers to trade. member states therefore play a key role, as do the national competition authorities. however, they need to be transparent in the decisions they take in that national context.

vice-president of the commission. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i think it is important to have a discussion this evening on the drugs monitoring centre in lisbon. it is a successful european agency, which has proved particularly useful in implementing our european strategy for drug prevention and control. i would just like to mention some figures on drug use in europe, which i think are very impressive. this is data coming from the monitoring centre in lisbon. every month, one and a half million european citizens use cocaine, nine and a half million use cannabis, and three million use the latter drug more or less regularly, that is to say daily. in addition, the use of mixed drugs, often combined with alcohol, is increasing sharply. finally, the use of ecstasy, which is unfortunately increasingly produced in europe, has also increased. over one million european citizens are regular users of ecstasy. i am glad that this proposal is based on article 152 of the treaty, which allows parliament to have a full role in this matter. i would like to thank the rapporteur, mrs brepoels, in particular for her report, which i consider to be important and balanced, and for her efforts to reach agreement on the commission's proposal at the first reading of the codecision procedure. i believe that the compromise amendments tabled by the rapporteur will be accepted, and that will make it possible to conclude this report successfully. i can give my favourable opinion on most of the amendments, with a few exceptions which i will mention briefly. my doubts are about amendments nos 8, 15, 18 and 32 to 39. i would just like to make a small reference to amendment no 15, which provides for a european parliament opinion on the centre's three-year work programme. the interinstitutional agreement provides for a legislative rather than an executive role for parliament, and i therefore believe that the drawing up of an opinion or the approval of the work programme of an executive centre would be a step away from the european parliament's role of political control. amendment no 18 deals with a matter which is a horizontal competence of the commission, and concerns the regulation of 18 european agencies. this amendment cannot be accepted not because of the merits of the matter, but because the search for political agreement on the horizontal proposal is under way. finally, amendments nos 32 to 39 give me cause for concern because they seem to me to be outside the scope of the compromise reached between the three institutions, since they provide for a specific evaluation role for the centre of lisbon which seems to me to be quite outside the role of the centre of lisbon. the main purpose of this centre is to provide tools to facilitate the evaluation and monitoring of the member states and the commission. i therefore think it is not advisable to provide for an independent evaluation role for the centre. apart from these objections, i support both the report and the rapporteur's amendments.
the proposal we are discussing today has a long history, as the legal debate dates back to the end of 2003, when the commission presented its first proposal. the parliament then issued an opinion in april 2004, but afterwards, following discussions in the council, it was decided to change the legal basis by which the codecision procedure was applied. the commission proposal covers a number of issues. it actually provides for the codification of three earlier amendments, but also for the extension of the role of the centre and the adjustment to the new european reality after the enlargement of the ten new countries. the centre will therefore focus more on new trends in drug use, such as the combination of legal and illegal psychoactive substances. the proposal also aims to make a number of changes to adapt the functioning of the centre's organs to day-to-day practice and, as i said, to the enlargement of the european union. we are convinced that each of the proposed changes will be beneficial to the efficiency of the centre. we support the demand for more objective and comparable data, the incorporation of the executive office in the centre's rules of procedure, the regular evaluation of the centre's activities and the reitox network of national focal points. finally, we think that the six-yearly external evaluations are very important. the commission's proposal is to be submitted to the council for adoption. parliament considered that the exchange of information on best practices in dealing with the drug problem could add value to member states' policies, since the centre's main task is to collect and disseminate information on drugs and drug use. as regards the composition of the executive committee, it seems appropriate to appoint, in addition to the chair and the vice-chair, two members of the management board representing the member states and two representatives of the commission. of course, decisions in this case should be taken by consensus. we also think it desirable that the president of the centre should be able to be heard by parliament in addition to the director of the centre. we also believe that a far-reaching cooperation between the national focal points and the organisations active in this field will enable rapid and adequate monitoring of the european drug situation and will enable the member states to respond more adequately to new developments and phenomena concerning drugs and drug use. the main points of discussion with the council in this dossier concerned the composition of the scientific committee and the procedure for appointing the director. the scientific committee is responsible for giving opinions on scientific questions concerning the centre's activities. parliament considered that the composition of this committee should be limited to a maximum of 15 members, selected on the basis of their expertise by an open procedure, and not to one representative from each member state, as was the case up to now. as the commissioner said, a proposal for the appointment and renewal of the director's term of office for all agencies is currently pending, it was decided to return to the provisions of the founding regulation and await the outcome of these negotiations. the commission has always sought to reach an agreement between the three institutions, including the council and the commission, at first reading. i am very glad that after many discussions we have succeeded in this. the compromise proposed takes over almost all the proposals of parliament and we are therefore convinced that nothing can mr president, mr president-in-office of the council, mr barroso, i would like to return to one aspect of your speech. you said, quite rightly, "you have to walk on two legs. on the one hand, the deepening of the single market, on the other, social solidarity". i would add: social justice and equity. i think, mr president of the commission, that rejection and europe-pessimism are fuelled by a fairly clear awareness that we are not exactly walking on two legs and that the action of your commission, like that of the council, has been to privilege the first leg. i would like to say or remind you that under your presidency, as under the prodi commission, no new social legislation has been decided by the european institutions. in addition, no new collective agreements between the social partners have been concluded. it is this profound imbalance, i believe, that is increasingly felt by a section of the population which, because of globalisation, is the most precarious. it seems to me that the council, parliament and the commission must respond to this situation. this brings me to the second point, which concerns instruments for solidarity or social justice. mr president of the commission, we cannot build a fair model tomorrow if we do not have a tax policy at european level. this is the contradiction between those who are reluctant to pursue enlargement and those who want more social justice: maintaining unanimity on any taxation issue is tantamount to blocking the possibility of a european taxation policy. it took 15 years to have a directive on savings income, which is also full of exceptions, and we are not making any progress on common standards for corporate taxation. in conclusion, mr president, the political project of the european union is not simply a single market, which we need, but competition between national models. we need more voluntarism and common policies at european level to guarantee fairness and a european model of society.

president. - mr weiler, in his book 'the constitution of europe' you have described in detail how the court of justice systematically extended the powers of the european institutions in the past without any democratic legitimacy and without any mandate from the governments of the member states. now, by virtue of that notorious judgment of 13 september 2005, criminal law is also a european competence. the court of justice is thus sweeping the floor with the limitations of jurisdiction of the european treaties. it seems that the court of justice is a kind of legal absurdistan with judges who are out of touch with the world and who are no longer accountable to anyone or anything. it is therefore surprising that our citizens, when given the opportunity, should express their dissatisfaction by voting against the european constitution, for example.
mr president, this month we have another report, this time by our colleague mr ždanoka, which is a report against racism and discrimination. again, a catalogue of politically correct clichés that are becoming a speciality of this institution. it is raining again with speeches of racism and discrimination and calls for european legislation and for the member states to be obliged to actually discriminate against european citizens in favour of foreigners, who, according to this report, should not only have the same political and social rights, but who should also have priority in many areas. it is clear that in this european parliament there is little common sense to see that racism in europe is fortunately a very marginal phenomenon, but that on the other hand our citizens are concerned about the erosion and the damage to the security, prosperity and cultural identity to which they are entitled.
i voted against the zdanoka report because, for the umpteenth time, we are faced with a series of proposals which do not solve the problem, but which are part of the problem. we need to stop treating minority groups like immigrants as pathetic. we must stop attributing all their problems in advance to discrimination. this left-wing discourse has only exacerbated the problems. we need a full course change. we must emphasize personal responsibility and the need for immigrants to adapt to our laws, our language, our standards and values. by always talking about so-called discrimination, as we do here in the european parliament, we weaken the impulse for foreigners to integrate. this may be good for the growing anti-discrimination industry, but it is bad for the immigrants and the country in which they have settled.
in writing. fight against all forms of discrimination, another example of the union's pioneering legislation in the social field. today, in response to a commission green paper, parliament is adopting an own-initiative report setting out its vision of the means to be used to combat discrimination, in society and in the workplace. in 2000, the eu adopted two directives (2000/43 and 2000/78) obliging the member states to have a legislative and judicial apparatus capable of stopping this phenomenon. while all national legislation has thus been considerably strengthened, there is still work to be done, particularly in the field of education. parliament therefore proposes a whole series of ways of combating the various forms of direct and indirect discrimination, whether based on ethnic origin, sex, age, disability, sexual orientation or religious beliefs. promote best practice to avoid the 'positive discrimination' or quota logic advocated by some. the union has few powers in social matters, but it is clear that it takes the lead when it has the means to do so. this is an ambitious text, an additional thumbs-up for those who denounce an anti-social europe.

in writing. in essence, i want to say that i refuse to bury the draft constitutional treaty today! no to the neurotic feeling of failure! let's give ourselves a chance to move forward. let us continue the ratification process so as to reach the 4/5th threshold set out in declaration 30. in that case, as we know, the european council will have to deal with the matter. it will then have much more information than it does today to examine, in particular: - whether and under what conditions ratification could be resumed in countries where referendums have been rejected; - how to safeguard at least those parts of the text which make the charter of fundamental rights legally binding, those which allow an enlarged europe to function better or those which enhance the union's social role. i do not believe that a majority of citizens are against these aspects of the text. this is certainly not to the liking of those who have mastered the art of doubt and demoralization. but personally, i want to revive the virtuous circle of the european integration process.

i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr costa, and the other shadow rapporteurs for their excellent cooperation. we agree on almost everything, except on a few minor points, or on the important issues, and we will be able to take a strong stand as a parliament. safety has, of course, been a more important issue since september 11th than ever before, and it stands to reason that the aviation sector demands our particular attention, given the potentially devastating effects of airstrikes. for my group, this means that we must always strive for the best possible security, but that this must be accompanied by sufficient transparency and respect for civil liberties. security efforts and related costs should be proportionate to the security risks. the committee of experts which adopts decisions implementing this framework legislation should not be given carte blanche to propose anything without a counter-opinion. amendment no 61 is therefore important, since it formally provides for the creation of a stakeholder advisory group. we believe that not only industry but also other organisations such as passenger associations and, where appropriate, organisations which can give advice on civil liberties should be able to take part. we also ask for an annual report on the application of this regulation. the second point on which we agree - the rapporteur has already referred to this - is that it must be made clear who is to bear the cost of airport security. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the safety of the road transport sector is not compromised. is it the users, is it the industry, is it the government? it will of course have to be a combination, but what is important is that we should pursue the same european rules of the game everywhere. we have already discussed this on another occasion, commissioner. you were supposed to come up with an initiative by the summer. we're looking forward to it. the commission proposal introduces a new chapter on safety on board civil aircraft. it includes the famous sky marshals, armed security officers on board airplanes. we are in principle against the presence of weapons on aircraft because misuse or accident can have catastrophic consequences and we are therefore not in favour of these measures. the problem is that the deployment of security agents is a matter for the member states and will remain so. some countries, such as france and britain, use them, others, such as greece and spain, do not. in these circumstances, we are pleased that this addition provides the opportunity to regulate this matter at least partly at european level, for example by imposing uniform procedures and strict safety requirements or by providing for common training. we have tabled two amendments ourselves. firstly, a formal amendment on gibraltar airport to bring the text into line with existing agreements. secondly, an amendment in which we call for the establishment of a european system for the certification of equipment used for airport security, such as x-ray scanners, etc. this is a matter for a european approach, if only because not all member states have the necessary knowledge to be able to make a good judgement of the increasingly modern security equipment. the confusion of standards is also a handicap for european industry compared with american industry. so i'd like your support on this, too.

president. - ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, a parliament has many responsibilities. not just making and simplifying laws, but also monitoring certain decisions and whether or not those decisions have been implemented correctly. the core of these control tasks is the control of the implementation of the budget. the commission has also been asked to examine whether the more than 100 billion euros used to implement european policies have been properly spent. to ensure that european taxpayers' money is used in a useful and efficient way and that it reaches the right hands. for the 2004 financial year, irregularities and fraud reported by the member states amounted to almost eur 1 billion. while we used to see that most of these reports concerned mainly agriculture and less structural policy expenditure, we now see, as mr bösch has rightly said, a reverse trend. more reports in the structural funds, less in agriculture. the fall in agricultural production is of course entirely due to the proper functioning of the integrated administration and control system. against this background - and i want to emphasize this - it is unacceptable that one member state, greece, is still in default seven years after the facts. mr bösch gives a good overview of what is wrong in his report and he does not hesitate to mention man and horse. he is right to use the highly efficient method of naming and shaming. in the past year, thanks in part to the support of commissioner callas, we have succeeded in forcing a number of member states to disclose the names of the final beneficiaries of agricultural payments. that gives us a good idea of how agricultural money is spent and a good idea of the anomalies. it has taught us, once again, that the money does not always go to the farmers, but to the big food giants. i think we should now go a step further and force the member states to also disclose the final beneficiaries of the structural funds, including the regional fund and the social fund. i would also like to take this debate to commend the people of olaf here. they're doing a great job. they get criticism sometimes. mr bösch rightly raises a number of criticisms in his report, but we must also bear in mind that enormous progress has been made in the last eight years. finally, a word on two very fragile areas, vat fraud and cigarette smuggling. vat fraud must be tackled and i welcome the commission's communication on this. as regards cigarette smuggling, i will be publishing an own-initiative report later this year. i am very pleased that, despite the fact that there is an own-initiative report, mr bösch has already devoted a number of paragraphs to this problem in his report. we have an agreement with philip morris. the money we get from that should be used for the anti-fraud policy. i think we should look together with the commission, as commissioner špidla rightly said, at how that money should be used, for example in the hercules programme. we will have to invest a lot of time and energy in this over the next few months.

mr president, madam commissioner, mr commissioner, mr minister, ladies and gentlemen, let me first of all recall the excellent spirit which prevailed in the committee on regional development when it considered the three proposals for amendments to the regulations which are now before the house. the narrow financial perspective for 2007-13 has not, i believe, prevented us from improving the legislative instruments for this reform, which for us european socialists is fundamental because it reflects the solidarity between the regions. our work has highlighted the need to support sustainable urban development in our regions, as jean-marie beaupuy has just mentioned. in this connection, it is worth noting the increasing concentration of europeans in cities. we have also succeeded in taking better account of the special situation of the regions affected by the statistical effect, which will be able to benefit from a transitional support mechanism. apart from the weakness of the budgetary basis of this reform, let me point out two disappointments. the first is a half-deception, since it concerns the partial eligibility of housing expenditure, which i fought hard for. this is welcome, but it is regrettable that it is only possible in the countries which joined after 1 may 2004. this national distinction is dangerous to me; it is also unfounded because the housing crisis is very present in some large cities in other member states. second disappointment: i do not understand why the period during which a company cannot move its activities abroad has been reduced when it has benefited from structural funds. this period was set at seven years in the commission's original proposal, which was confirmed by the vote in this parliament, and in view of recent events, particularly the loss of jobs caused by relocations in europe, i would ask the commission to review the report on relocations adopted by this parliament in march.
in writing. every year, nearly 800 000 tonnes of car batteries, 190 000 tonnes of industrial batteries and accumulators and 160 000 tonnes of portable batteries are placed on the eu market. these batteries contain heavy metals (mercury, lead, cadmium) which are harmful to the environment and human health. however, currently only six member states have a national collection system for the recycling of used batteries. among these, belgium is a good student: its collection rate is close to 60%! i am in favour of the directive we are about to vote on, which specifically aims to organise such a system throughout the eu by 2008. some of the proposed measures are particularly worthy of our support: a general ban on the placing on the market of batteries or accumulators containing an excessive proportion of heavy metals; the setting of quantified collection and recycling targets; the obligation to mention the actual lifetime on the label in order to inform the consumer; support for research into the development of more environmentally friendly batteries and new recycling techniques.
in writing. in the context of my colleague alain lamassoure's very technical report on the union's own resources, i think it is essential to recall the idea put forward by belgium and guy verhofstadt a few years ago to give the union genuine financial autonomy, by means of what has been called the introduction of a european tax. an additional tax? i'm sure you didn't. simply, instead of paying to the national state what we have to pay to europe, allow direct funding by the european citizen, like the american citizen vis-à-vis the federal state. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. i would remind you that europe costs 26 cents a day to every european! while the project is welcomed by some member states (austria, luxembourg), others remain wary of an instrument designed to bring europe closer to europeans. this would also help us to get out of the sterile debate between net contributors and net beneficiaries. europe will then certainly have the means to achieve its ambitions!
in writing. more than a year ago, with 13 of my belgian colleagues in the european parliament, i signed a white paper in la libre belgique calling on the 25 heads of state and government to go beyond the objectives of the kyoto protocol. among the proposals put forward was the priority to include emissions from the aviation and shipping sectors in future climate protection plans. it is in this sense that i welcome the ambitious vote today on the lucas report which provides for a whole series of measures to combat the environmental impacts of aviation, and in particular the end of the vat exemption on aviation fuel. the current situation is worrying: emissions from aviation are destroying a quarter of the poor results achieved in the fight against greenhouse gases. i want to be clear: it is not a question of taxing everything, but of putting an end to a distortion which does not provide sufficient incentives for the aviation sector to invest in new technologies, and whose first victim is our environment. global warming is the major challenge of the 21st century, europe must continue to lead by example!
for the greens/efa group mr president, commissioner, i have listened to you attentively. i also read in the news agencies that the commission had stated that the process of restructuring and relocation carried out by opel was considered normal and complied with the rules of the various european funds. i do think, and you have made your point quite clearly, that it is a little pointless for parliament and for some of our colleagues to repeat for months and months criticisms of global capitalism or total capitalism: we live in a world where competition is getting tougher and where the future of the automobile sector is probably more determined by renault-nissan-gm alliances as announced than by transactions we are talking about today. that said, if we are to have a role, i would like you to be able to answer four specific questions as a commissioner responsible for these matters in the commission: the first is that we have always advocated the idea of the european works council and industrial administration observatories to anticipate this type of change. in this parliament we wanted to strengthen the provisions of the european works council type. in this context, do you not think that the commission should take initiatives to strengthen this legislation? second question: the barroso commission has proposed an adjustment fund for globalisation-related restructuring. i do not know whether this is the case for opel, but what exactly is it? can you assure us that this fund will be used to help workers who have been penalised by this type of operation during a transitional period, so that they can rebuild their professional future through training measures? thirdly, you have rightly referred to the cars 21 programme, to the commission's attention to research and development programmes and to the cars of the future. if we consider - and i am not the first to do so - that the car is a sustainable development for the 21st century, especially for our european cities, it is nevertheless confusing to note that, given that the sustainable car for the next 25 years will have to have a hybrid engine, like the toyota prius, that european manufacturers are still lagging behind in this area. what is your assessment of the research and development programmes in this field and the marketing of this type of product? finally, fourth point: it is quite clear - and the example of opel is obviously shocking in this respect - that there is competition between neighbouring countries: portugal, spain. do you not think that, while respecting the rules of competition, it is the duty of the commission and the european union to impose common minimum rules on companies operating in the community, and i think that this would be a panacea for harmonizing corporate taxation? these are four types of action which the commission, as guardian of the treaties and promoter of the common european interest, should encourage. in this respect, i regret to say to you, commissioner, that we still find your commission too weak and too unambitious on the various points i have just mentioned.

i think that the time has come to take a step forward in this matter. it is something we have been working on for a long time and we are now close to an agreement which will enable us to achieve a high level of safety and fairer competition between airlines. the question now is whether the compromise on the table is a good compromise. we all know - and it has been said a number of times - that it is not ideal, but sometimes the best is the enemy of the good. but right now, we have to take what we can get, because there is a compromise on the table: no downward levelling, the demand for a cabin crew regime, the night flight regime for freight carriers, the results of the easa investigation as soon as possible, after 2 years instead of 3 years. now we must seize the opportunity to move forward in two steps. firstly, we must approve the compromise now and i am glad to hear what gilles savary has just said, namely that if the compromise is approved, we will not go any further. if we have accepted that compromise, we will have a european basis on which we can work, on which the commission can start working, but for everyone and not for certain member states which can afford to make higher demands. that's the first step. the second step is that once we have that european regulation, we will improve it and work on certain points to raise the level for everyone - and again, not for certain member states that can afford it. that's the way we have to work, in those two steps. if we miss that first step, if we don't compromise now, we can't move forward. i understand very well the criticisms and the questions raised by some people in certain categories of staff. it's in the interest of safety that they ask those questions. but i asked them: "would you rather have this than nothing?" and every time they would say, "no, no, we want something". i think the rapporteur has done everything possible to push all the boundaries as far as possible. the opportunity we now have to reach a compromise and to start with a european settlement is an opportunity we must seize in any case. if we reject that compromise, i am convinced, together with the shadow rapporteurs and the rapporteur, that we will be left with nothing and that we will be far from the goal we want to achieve. i would like to express my gratitude to the rapporteur and to the commissioner for the work he has done. i regret that the council does not want to go further on a number of points and that it is stuck in a kind of reasoning which i do not understand. but with the adoption of the compromise we have worked out, there is in any case no risk of ending up with no result and making the situation worse rather than better. so thanks to mr savary, thanks to all the other colleagues, i think we'll have a good result tomorrow.

in writing. today we are voting on a proposal for a decision on the strengthening of crossborder police cooperation at events involving large numbers of people from several member states, for example at major sporting events with an international dimension (football world cup, olympic games, cycling tours, etc.), but also at religious events, state visits, international political meetings or summits. experience has clearly shown that cooperation, trust, proper communication between the police services concerned, the availability of reliable information and good preparation were essential elements for the successful conduct of such events, especially with a view to prevention. the text takes account of each of these elements and provides very specific next steps. before the events: an annual planning of the expected needs. during events: cross-border cooperation and assistance between national police services. finally, after the events: an evaluation and, if necessary, proposals for adapting european legislation (in particular the schengen convention). at the time of the second semi-final of a world cup which has so far gone without major incident, we can only support this text with the hope that it will be as often as possible.
president, our ad hoc committee has failed to find any evidence that the cia has engaged in torture or related illegal activities in europe. we have not gone beyond suspicions, clues, and even insinuations. some of my colleagues on the left wanted to use the activities of the temporary committee to promote cheap anti-americanism. this biased report is an expression of that. the way in which the committee has operated so far also raises questions. witnesses were sometimes accompanied by lawyers who prevented difficult questions from being answered. it has happened many times that the vagueness of the persons involved has prevented us from having a clear picture of the facts of which they are accused or of their political background. other people who appeared before the committee, such as high representative javier solana, were so predictably unremarkable that their appearance was a waste of time for everyone. if the investigation is to be continued, it would be better if it were carried out by the national authorities which are competent to do so.
member of the commission. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i am pleased to address you today at this last plenary session before the summer recess. this is also our last meeting before the international aids conference in toronto in august. as you know, toronto will provide the commission with a leading forum to express its vision of the hiv/aids epidemic. the european commission is obviously still very concerned about the problem of hiv/aids. despite our efforts, the plague continues to spread around the world. it now affects tens of millions of people, infecting more and more girls and young women. the countries most affected are still the poorest countries in sub-saharan africa, where the prevalence rate is not decreasing and where the disease continues to undermine any prospects for human, social and economic development. in the face of this tragedy, the european commission is more determined than ever to give its full support to its partners in africa and elsewhere. our goal is to give them the tools to fight the pandemic, by effectively combining prevention, treatment and care, but beyond this combination, we remain convinced that hiv/aids cannot be treated in isolation. firstly, the fight against hiv/aids cannot be separated from the fight against other poverty-related diseases, including malaria and tuberculosis. secondly, hiv/aids brings to the forefront in a brutal way all the major development challenges: limited access to social services, widening inequalities - social and gender - poor governance and corruption, and persisting extremely low levels of economic and technological development. in short, the glaring deficit of the state in its essential royal functions. hiv/aids can only be successfully combated if the leaders of developing countries address all these issues simultaneously. this integrated approach to hiv/aids is the driving principle of community action, and it is also at the heart of a concerted approach with the member states, but also with our other partners in the international community. the main financial instrument used by the commission is the global fund to fight hiv/aids, tuberculosis and malaria, which devotes almost 57% of its resources to the fight against hiv/aids. we preferred the global fund because it is currently the fastest and most effective instrument for supporting the integrated approaches developed by the beneficiary countries. i think you know that the commission plays a key role in this fund, as it is the vice-president. since the global fund was set up at the end of 2001, the commission has contributed €522 million, or 11% of total contributions. if we take the member states of the european union into account, we are obviously by far the largest donor to the fund, with almost 60% of the fund's total. i must say that i was involved a few months ago in the replenishment of this fund in london, and i do not hide from you that we were a little disappointed at a certain lukewarmness, at any rate a certain reserve, in replenishing this fund. we're a long way from where we set out to be. so there is still a lot of work to be done to mobilise donors. so we are implementing a series of instruments, projects and financial support programmes, in which general budget support has a privileged place. this approach is in line with our priority of making our partners owners of their own policies and thus their own destiny. in this spirit, the commission has committed almost €2.4 billion since 2002 to the general budget for africa alone, where the impact of hiv/aids is by far the most severe. we are of course in continuous political dialogue with our partners on the use of these funds. this dialogue also aims to encourage them to implement an integrated approach - including the inclusion of human rights and good governance issues. i am also thinking of gender equality, discrimination and violence against women, and the issue of girls' access to education. in addition to these efforts through the global fund and bilateral action, the commission is actively involved in international forums dealing with poverty-related diseases - i am thinking in particular of the world trade organisation, the united nations general assembly special session on hiv/aids, the g8, and conferences such as the one in toronto. the commission has played a key role in ensuring that the european union speaks with one voice in these bodies on this issue. in addition, the commission has also been engaged for several years in a dialogue with the pharmaceutical industry to advocate the provision of low-cost medicines for developing countries. this dialogue has borne fruit: the introduction of a tiered pricing mechanism has reduced the price of certain medicines by 95%. this system allows developing countries to pay a price that is commensurate with their means, while protecting the companies that produce these drugs from having to reimport their products into the highly lucrative markets of developed countries. in 1999, a full course of antiviral treatment for hiv cost $10,000, today it costs $200. we are ourselves research actors, working with industry and research centres in the member states, and our efforts are particularly focused on microbicides and vaccines. we have also launched a partnership bringing together member states' research programmes to speed up clinical trials and thus ensure faster market access for medicines. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, we must take the fight against hiv/aids further, to make prevention and treatment available to all those who need it by 2010. in this respect, we are following very closely the implementation by some member states of innovative financing mechanisms, in particular the air ticket tax. we also welcome the growing contributions of private foundations, such as the bill gates foundation, to the fight against hiv/aids, tuberculosis and malaria. this creativity in solutions and the growing involvement of the private sector give me hope. they reflect the growing awareness of the scourge of hiv/aids and the need to respond to it in a proactive manner to safeguard our common future. we must not let our guard down, we must continue our efforts without letup. we can achieve the ultimate goal of providing care to all who need it, and seeing future generations grow up without the fear of aids. i look forward to continuing to work with the european parliament to make this a reality. thank you for your attention.
on behalf of the pse group thank you, mr president, and thank you, mr commissioner, for being here at this somewhat hallucinatory night session with the few of us who are involved in this debate. i hope you have enjoyed a very interesting match between france and portugal. i also thank you for your statement, commissioner. the socialist group was relieved when the un high level meeting on their commitment to a global and coherent policy on hiv/aids. the european union must therefore also make a contribution and contribute. i would like to hear from you four specific things, commitments in fact, that the pse group would like to have on the agenda in toronto. firstly, we think that the 6% for human and social development in the new development cooperation instrument is a little too care. as the un aids report says, we know what needs to be done to stop aids. what we need now is the political will and also the financial means to do it.
i have already pointed out, ladies and gentlemen, in the committee on development and cooperation, what i find most disappointing about this report. i would also like to say the same thing in the debate in the hemisphere. nowhere is there any mention of the vague social consequences of migration for the eu countries, which are already struggling with problems of ghettoisation, crime, uprooting and islamism. i would have expected a french rapporteur to give some thought to this after the experience of the french suburbs in the autumn of last year. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject of the future of the internal market. that's too human. but michel rocard, the former chairman of the committee on development cooperation in this parliament, was right when he once said as a socialist prime minister of france and as a member of the same party as the rapporteur: "france cannot absorb the misery of the whole world". europe cannot absorb the misery of the whole world. the european union is not only a political force, but also a political force, and it is a political force, and it is a political force. because they're left even poorer. and fun but unrealistic ideas like brain mobility and circular migration are not going to change that. more immigration into our countries will therefore mean more misery in the developing countries, contrary to what this report suggests. europe needs an absolute stop to immigration. the developing countries need effective aid on the ground.
i have already asked commissioner frattini some questions in the past on the mutual information file on asylum and immigration, because i wonder what the real added value of this system is in the fight against illegal immigration. i'm not saying it's a bad thing, but i think it's a patch on a wooden leg. the real problem is that the regularization policies of italy, spain and belgium have led to an influx of hundreds of thousands of new economic success seekers, who are spreading across europe without any problems because of our open borders policy. if the european union really intends to tackle the problem of illegal immigration, it must start at the beginning. the only way to counteract the suction effect of this policy is, of course, to reject the nefarious regularizations outright, followed immediately by a consistent return policy for all illegal and criminal aliens.

in writing. we voted for the epp resolution and rejected the joint resolution on the swift case as a whole because of the imbalance, which cannot be remedied by amendments. we all know how difficult it is to draw the line between the vital priorities of the fight against terrorism and the equally fundamental respect for our individual freedoms. the debate is fuelled by the successive detours of the conditions of detention in guantanamo, the case of the secret cia flights, the detention centers in europe... and now this swift case. so it's a delicate border, but a debate of vital importance in a world where terrorism now knows no borders. an investigation is underway in belgium to determine any possible violation of our data protection laws. we do not think that this vindictive resolution - if not downright anti-american in form, angelic in substance (4 and 13: a summit! the secret services should announce their operations publicly!) and often illegible - improves our image with the europeans. there were other ways to express our concern to shed light on a possible offence, while recalling our visceral commitment to the fight against those whose ideology undermines our values.

president. - the next item is the joint debate on the following motions for resolutions: more and more observers seem to be assuming it. the commission has not taken any further steps, particularly since hong kong nine months ago, and in july the negotiations were postponed until stuttgart. but like the commissioner, like most of my colleagues here, i refuse to believe that this is the beginning of the end. after all, there is a lot at stake. failure to do so could lead to the collapse of the multilateral trading system with all its consequences. the absence of a global trade agreement increases the risk that more and more countries will resort to bilateral or regional agreements, which will in any case weaken the position of poor countries. the breakdown of negotiations could also increase trade disputes, with wto members trying to achieve through a procedural layer what they failed to achieve through an agreement. but there is more, and the commissioner has pointed out: the wto itself is in danger of being called into question sooner or later. the strengthening of the wto's transparency and democratic legitimacy is therefore more important than ever. it is essential for our group that rich and more developed countries try to reconcile their positions in advance. there is no point in holding another high mass if the us and the eu do not close the gap between them in their agricultural and other offers. the us congress recently applauded the stubborn attitude of us negotiators. such a blame game does not contribute to constructive solutions. all the main players, including the eu, the us and the g20, must make efforts to achieve results. in the meantime, the so-called hong kong acquis must be maintained in its entirety. all pro-development commitments must be respected. whether there is an agreement or not, the eu must not back down on its intention to end export subsidies by 2015. at the same time, all developed countries and advanced developing countries should be invited to follow the eu's 'everything but arms' initiative. the tone of the compromise resolution before us, mr president, is voluntarist and positive. it is a reaffirmation of our belief
