in writing. parliament rightly reiterates today that multilingualism is an expression of the cultural and linguistic diversity of the union, which must be preserved. parliament rightly states that citizens have the right to communicate with the european institutions in all the official languages of the union. parliament stressed once again that elected representatives have the right to speak their own language at all official ep meetings. the fact is that knowledge of languages can never be an additional condition for election. i pay tribute to the many hundreds of interpreters who provide us with a brilliant and high-quality interpretation day after day. the fact that all this costs money is a normal price for the functioning of european democracy. i agree with the proposals to save where possible by discouraging very late cancellations and applications. the fact that the average cost of interpreting in the ep is higher than in the council or the commission is also explained by our forced monthly move to strasbourg, where very few local interpreters can be used, so that the cost of interpreting in the ep in strasbourg increases by 13%. so saving can also be stopped by this senseless monthly move.
in writing. daphne, a nice name for the european programme to combat violence against women and children. the programme, which is celebrating its tenth anniversary, is making good progress, with more than 350 projects financed, mainly by ngos active in this sector. violence against women is a scourge without a name. it is a matter of concern that in 2006 adult women still appear in the category of "vulnerable persons". worldwide, one in three women is estimated to have been a victim of one of the many forms of violence. to combat this barbarity, several weapons are needed: a draconian legislative arsenal, zero tolerance, listening to the victims and awareness campaigns. children are also affected. the very appearance of the pnvd, a dutch political party which campaigns for child pornography and adult-child sex, shows the flaws in our system and says a lot about the reforms that need to be carried out. in ten years, daphne's budget has increased fivefold to $25 million a year. i believe that this is still not enough to combat this form of violence, which is too often trivialised and which affects the most vulnerable: women and children.
in writing. the present proposal to strengthen the daphne programme for the period 2007-13 deserves our full support. in my own country, we have been confronted on several occasions in recent years with unacceptable physical, sexual and psychological violence against children, young people and women. the recent murder of two innocent young children in liège makes us shudder at these perfidious acts. organisations such as child focus, the european federation for missing and sexually exploited children, the european network of children's ombudsmen (enoc) and the many national and operational ngos are doing a great job. authors like chris de stoop showed us the raw reality of human trafficking and forced prostitution. despite the efforts made, there is still a lot of work to be done. it is also becoming increasingly clear that more attention will have to be paid to the situation of street children, who are not only victims of drug and human traffickers but also suffer from violence and sexual abuse. the daphne programme is therefore ensuring that europe is not a bed-and-breakfast, but offers concrete help to the most vulnerable in our society. we must all continue to fight against those who immorally exploit, humiliate and even kill the weakest of the weak. human dignity is above all else.
member of the commission. the birds directive generally prohibits spring hunting. it allows this only under certain conditions, the main one being that there are no satisfactory alternatives. so far, several member states which have applied for such derogations have not been able to demonstrate that there are no satisfactory solutions for hunting, for example in the autumn season. the reason hunting is prohibited in the spring is because wild birds migrate to breeding grounds. they are birds that have survived the winter and many other hardships and are going to the places where they breed their kind. it is therefore a major reason why hunting of wild birds is not allowed at this time. in particular, as regards malta: we have already requested, in the autumn of 2004, data from the maltese government on birds hunted in the 2004 period. the maltese authorities have sent us the relevant data for the 2004 spring hunting season. from this information, which the commission has carefully assessed by examining whether the justification for the derogation, namely that there is no satisfactory solution for hunting in other seasons, is indeed present, we have concluded that there are no such conditions. therefore, given that there is a significant number of watermelons and eggs in the autumn period, which is not far from the number of birds hunted in the spring period, and in accordance with the judgments of the european court of justice, we conclude that there is a satisfactory solution and therefore the derogation is not allowed. the commission, therefore, following this finding, in july 2006 initiated legal proceedings against malta for infringement of the birds directive by allowing the spring hunting of these two species. i should like to say that similar infringement proceedings have been brought against other member states on the basis of the derogation sought, for which the necessary conditions were not met, and that there have been convictions, such as recently in spain and finland, precisely for this reason.
member of the commission. i know that there is doubt about the data submitted to us by the maltese authorities, but even with these data, we have concluded that there is no reason for a derogation from the ban on spring hunting and we have started infringement procedures against malta. what more can we do? we want to show everybody that we are very serious about applying and enforcing the birds directive, which includes that provision. otherwise, if we give different signals, many other countries would think that we have watered down the provisions of the birds directive and might also ask for a derogation. so we have started the infringement procedures and we shall see whether the maltese authorities will comply and ban hunting during the spring period.
president, commissioner, we are talking about 2004, but i read in the maltese press that there was also a lot of hunting of migratory birds in the spring of 2005 and the spring of 2006 and that quite a few birds were shot. can you tell me whether the maltese authorities have already sent the commission an exceptional report on that spring 2005 hunt, as they are required to do? i think this was supposed to happen by june of this year. if so, can you tell us what the report says, roughly, what the reasons for the exception are? if not, when do you expect the report?
vice-president of the commission. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your report. the commission shares the grave concerns expressed by mr cornillet, and my assessment of the profoundly criminal nature of these forgeries is the same. the question rightly raises three areas of concern: firstly, the fight against counterfeit medicines within the european union. the commission is working on two levels. we have, of course, first strengthened legal protection. in 2004, new legislation on intellectual property protection was adopted, and in 2003 and 2004 new legislation on the treatment of goods suspected of infringing intellectual property rights by customs authorities. two proposals concerning criminal measures to enforce intellectual property rights are currently under discussion. a recent investigation in the community has revealed that in the last five years, 27 counterfeit medicines have appeared in legal distribution channels and 170 counterfeit medicines in illegal distribution channels within the european union. on the basis of this information, member states are currently developing measures to combat such counterfeiting. the commission, for its part, is examining, together with the member states, the european medicines agency and international partners, the future measures needed to protect public health. it is a question of enforcing existing legislation, of cooperation between the various bodies, of communication and awareness-raising. i do not rule out the possibility that the law in force needs to be developed further and that we need to talk about changes. the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the draft directive on the protection of the health of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos at work. the commission's recently published communication on a customs response to recent developments in counterfeiting and piracy of products addresses the problem of counterfeit medicines and recommends concrete measures to counter this growing threat. the experience of customs authorities has shown that a large proportion of counterfeits are transported in transit through the eu and are often destined for developing countries. the fight against imported counterfeits is particularly difficult because they often enter the european union in small quantities and customs have to detect the illegal goods in a very large number of small consignments. the commission encourages rightholders to make full use of the protection granted by council regulation (ec) no 1383/2003. when rightholders request that customs take action, they provide the customs authorities with the information they need to target the highest risk consignments. the second complex of issues concerns the question: what are we doing for the poorer countries, which are indeed the main victims of these falsified medicines? what are we doing to help them regulate and monitor the quality of medicines in their own territory? we are naturally committed to working at multilateral level. the commission therefore supports the initiatives of the world health organization to combat counterfeit medicines. the who conference in rome in february 2006 provided a platform for concrete action. the commission is supporting the establishment of a who task force on the international fight against falsified medicines. the results of the work of this task force will benefit all who members, including developing countries. the commission will actively participate in this task force and provide the necessary information. the commission also implements projects funded by the european development fund, in particular the ec-who partnership on pharmaceutical policy. the aim of this partnership is to enable acp countries to make their legislation and quality assurance systems for medicinal products more effective and to improve, inter alia, their controls, their action against defective or falsified medicinal products and their information management. eur 2.3 million has been allocated to this. the third question is the idea of an international convention to combat the counterfeiting of medicines. the idea of an international convention to combat falsification of medicines was discussed at length in the run-up to the who conference in february 2006 and at the conference itself. the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the draft directive on the protection of consumers from the risks of counterfeiting. the task force set up at the conference is better able to gather and share information and to develop the urgently needed solutions. in conclusion, let me reassure you that the commission shares parliament's concerns and is ready to respond to suggestions and take further initiatives.
on behalf of the alde group. mr president, commissioner, i would like to thank my colleague, mr thierry cornillet, for having opened this essential debate. we all agree that pirated drugs - i completely agree with what he said - are a killer scourge, especially, of course, in the least developed countries. this potentially lethal counterfeit is, unfortunately, little known, and almost silent in most cases. this is far from the spectacular and media-oriented actions which are being carried out, and which have also been mentioned in some cases, by the manufacturers of luxury goods and which - it should also be noted - have led to mediocre results. antibiotics, the pill, steroids, baby powder, anti-malarials - we've already talked about them - vaccines - a fake tamiflu, for example - viagra too, the fake drug industry copies everything and immorally, because it does it without the knowledge of the consumer. thierry cornillet has cited a whole series of encouraging figures. i had noted the 25 billion euros a year that this trade represents. one in three antibiotics and one in ten medicines worldwide are fake. beyond these damaging figures, of course, for the economy, the issue here is the disastrous impact of this counterfeiting in terms of health. at best, these fakes are inactive, at worst, as i said, they kill, especially when it comes to drugs used in the treatment of deadly diseases like malaria, tuberculosis or aids. in china alone, the hub and empire of all sorts of counterfeiting, 200,000 people would die every year after being treated with counterfeit medicines, including in some hospitals that are run by the chinese red cross. these are the findings that can be made. the causes are known, at least in part. thierry cornillet has spoken at length about them: the lack, until recently, of real will on the part of national governments to combat this scourge, the absence of legislation in many countries to act as a real deterrent, the ridiculous cost of these counterfeits and, above all, the poverty in the countries concerned, the inability of the most vulnerable populations to access health systems, their desperate need for affordable medicines. in a debate like this, no one claims to have the solution or a ready-made solution, but it is necessary to propose a responsible policy in the face of this emergency. in april, when he spoke to this house about all forms of counterfeiting, commissioner frattini raised the issue of sanctions against europeans and developing countries. the european union must play a leading role, in close cooperation with the who, and put in place a comprehensive strategy to combat this scourge effectively. we must establish judicial and police coordination and, of course, provide for sanctions, but also for information and, above all, for prevention. the challenge today is to make essential, good quality medicines available to all the population in the countries concerned.

member of the commission. mr president, firstly i should like to express my thanks to the rapporteur mrs fraga estévez and the committee on fisheries and to mrs ries and the committee on the environment, public health and food safety for a constructive report on a community approach to eco-labelling for fisheries products. i am pleased at the high degree of interest they have shown in the commission’s communication. in fact, the report is a fitting and valuable contribution to the debate on eco-labelling. it contains many elements and ideas that are helpful to strengthen further the debate. the commission indeed attaches great importance to the development of a consistent policy on eco-labelling and to a consistent integration of environmental concerns into the common fisheries policy. together with other actions proposed by the commission, such as the implementation of sustainability which leads to sustainable yields, or the promotion of more environment-friendly fishing methods, i see eco-labelling as a complementary tool for a more environment-friendly fisheries policy. at the same time it satisfies the interests of both consumers and the industry. i would like to thank mr morillon for his description of eco-labelling as a positive sanction supporting sustainable behaviour. eco-labelling is a relatively new approach in the context of sustainable fisheries management. it is based on a voluntary and participative approach, through which fishermen will in future, within the limits of the standards set by legislation, define requirements and implement them. such a voluntary approach has been adopted in other fields, particularly in that of food safety with the enforcing of the haccp system, and has proved highly successful. i thank you for lending your support to that approach. i am happy to see that the commission and parliament share the same views on the overall objectives of eco-labelling schemes, on the principles and features that credible schemes should contain, and on the need to address properly a complex and currently rather anarchic reality. traceability, transparency, a sound scientific basis, and third-party certification are essential. i see these as the prerequisites that will help us make the most of eco-labelling schemes and that will contribute to the sustainability of fisheries. in the commission we opted for launching a debate on the right approach rather than proposing operational provisions at too early a stage. i understand that many of you feel that the commission should have been more assertive. however, i am convinced that it was wise to proceed as we did. due to the originality of the approach, the ambitious objective we have and the controversy that issues like these invariably raise, it would have been premature to bring forward an operational proposal as early as june last year. i assure you that our choice to launch a debate first has proven to be successful. it has facilitated and accelerated the exchange of views and relevant experience between all sides concerned. as a result we now have a sound and almost complete picture of the situation, on which we can base further community action. the active participation of the community in the fao debate has given us an early advantage, and by launching the debate and participating in fruitful exchanges of views we have strengthened our leading position in this matter. the definition of the notion of sustainability is at the heart of the debate. many of the parties involved have inquired about relevant criteria. to follow up on this, dg fisheries has set up a group of independent experts to work on that very issue. the expert group is on the point of concluding its work. based on that work the commission will draft guidelines for the setting-up of eco-labelling schemes and will propose a list of minimum requirements against which private eco-labelling schemes could be assessed. i have taken note of the resolution’s request for a communication to be addressed to the council and to parliament within the next six months. i have no difficulty in reverting to parliament. however, at this point in time, the shape and form of the commission’s proposals have still to be determined. looking at the broader picture, i believe it would be more appropriate to come back to parliament once the whole consultation exercise has been concluded. that would allow us to carry out a thorough assessment of the following discussions and consultations: a second shareholder consultation, to be held before the end of this year, which would be an opportunity to gather views on the future guidelines; further discussions in the council in the months to come, which will conclude the current discussion phase; and, additionally, the report from the expert group, which will form the basis for the preparation of operational provisions that could be tabled during the course of next year. all this will give us a further opportunity to discuss the future community approach towards eco-labelling on a consolidated and even more informed basis. that whole process should lead to the adoption of specific and concrete decisions. with regard to amendments 1 and 2, the commission believes that eco-labelling is a tool that will contribute to a more sustainable exploitation of fish stocks and improve the effectiveness of the common fisheries policy. with regard to amendment 3, it reiterates some of the advantages of adopting the third option for establishing minimum requirements for voluntary eco-labelling schemes, as mentioned in the commission communication. i thank you for this report and the wider debate generated on eco-labelling. i am confident that at the end of this process we can put into operation a proper basis for eco-labelling schemes that should have far-reaching and positive effects.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's environmental policy is not distorted. mr president, commissioner, i would like to begin by congratulating our rapporteur, mrs fraga, on the quality of her report, although it is nuanced, and it reflects the complexity of the issues at stake. the overall objective is to encourage the sector to adopt fishing methods that are more economically, socially and environmentally sustainable. the fact is that the over-exploitation of fisheries resources affects 25% of species and for some of them, in a particularly worrying way. for cod alone, stocks are at one third of the minimum recommended by scientific experts to avoid the extinction of the species. the committee on the environment has thus emphasized its priorities in its opinion. we are reluctant to the commission's preferred option in this debate, namely to establish minimum requirements - the commissioner has recalled this - for these eco-labelling programmes. our choice is more ambitious: a single and centralised labelling, essential criteria: transparency, optional nature of the programme, accessibility and credibility; the rapporteur has stressed this point. the success of this initiative depends on effective consultation with the industry, but ultimately it depends mainly on the consumer's choice. in this respect, a visit to the fish department of a supermarket is quite telling, i did it myself: "protect the sea", "support sustainable fishing", "fish ecologically", etc. there is a profusion of messages that create confusion. if labels are multiplied, we risk missing the point. the consumer will not feel involved, or pressured into making this better informed choice that we wish. we will not have achieved our objective of creating a virtuous circle. i therefore agree with mrs fraga's conclusions and hope that the commission will finally give serious consideration to the analysis and impact of the options proposed and that it will take our recommendations into account in the next steps of this process.
the concern about the civil liberties of europeans which emerges from the debate is of course touching and also justified. i would like to take this opportunity to say that i do not hear this fundamental defence of civil liberties when, for example, in my own country, belgium, the telephones of democratically elected members of my party are openly tapped by the state security, as was the case recently with the telephone of our leader of the group in the flemish parliament, filip de winter. if the civil liberties and privacy of elected members of parliament are being violated in the heart of the european union, in brussels, then i regret that i do not hear all those colleagues who are standing here leaping to tap the big evil devil in washington on the fingers. so let's not be the hapless virgins. of course, the rights of europeans and travellers must be protected as much as possible, but anyone who, after the foiled terrorist attacks this summer, still doubts the usefulness of much stricter control measures in international air traffic is actually almost criminally naive. it is unfortunately the price we have to pay for more security and for more freedom.
i voted for the belder report and i would like to congratulate the rapporteur on the work he has done. the socialist group's position is actually an argument for voting in favour of the report, because the criticism was that the report pays too much attention to the human rights situation in china. the fact is that the situation has not improved at all in recent years, not even since the tiananmen square events, and what we find is that there is too much willingness on the part of the governments in the european union, on the part of the business community and other bodies in europe, to simply trade with a communist regime in china, without there being any obligation to improve the human rights situation.
in writing. i support the compromise text on the middle east. there is no military solution to the problems in this region. the gross and deliberate use of violence by israel, in particular the bombing of civilian targets, the use of illegal weapons, the widespread destruction of civilian infrastructure and the serious pollution of coastal areas, as well as the indiscriminate rocket attacks by hezbollah, deserve our condemnation. member states should prevent a new arms race in the region by strictly complying with the eu code of conduct on arms exports for all arms supplies to the region. after the failure of us policy in the region, it is important to call for a new regional peace conference - a madrid ii - to find a comprehensive, lasting and viable solution, based on the right of the state of israel to live within secure and recognized borders and the right of the palestinians to a viable state based on the occupied territories, with extensive consideration of the security and disarmament aspects. the union should resume dialogue with syria and involve it in peace efforts, including through the signing of the joint association agreement as an important step towards a more effective approach to human rights in syria.

president, mr president of the commission, when decisions are taken with a market-correcting aim or effect, we know that we always come into politically sensitive waters in this house. and when we want to apply the principles of subsidiarity and proportionality in concrete situations, there is often the same sensitivity and the ideological impact is never far away. the rapporteur, mr rapkay, has nevertheless succeeded in producing a balanced report, and i should like to congratulate him sincerely on this. i am very pleased to see that, firstly, the importance of the availability of services of general interest is underlined and, secondly, that what is also stated in the common position on the services directive is confirmed, namely that it is for the member states to determine what services of general interest are. thirdly, i welcome the fact that the question of legal certainty is prominently present. i have no problem with what some here call the rapporteur's ambiguous language. i would rather call that a wise approach, perhaps a diplomatic approach, but at least one that can help us further along the road to solutions. there is no explicit call for a framework directive. the sectoral differences are recognised in this report. if this report is adopted, we will be making it clear that we attach more importance to the substance of the matter than to the debate on form. and that's what we have to be about. so let us stick to that rapkay report, the commission will know where a large majority in parliament is, where there are still differences. we can then, pending the outcome of the commission's communication, take steps towards a balanced approach which the citizens can expect from us.
mr president, mr president of the commission, i too am delighted and thank you, mr barroso, for being here. we have been discussing for years a european legislation for services of general economic interest across sectors. the european parliament spoke in favour of such legislation in 2001 and 2004 and the european commission - that was the prodi commission - did not follow up, making the legal argument of the lack of a legal basis and the political argument of the lack of the political majority in the council needed to make progress. on this last point, the reference to article 322 of the the internal market in services which partially covers services of general economic interest, a directive which many experts predict will not prevent many cases before the court of justice and which it is to be feared will undermine the achievement of the objective of greater legal certainty in the internal market in services. today, as other members have said, we are again faced with a european tell the commission and, ultimately, the court what kind of general provisions they want for european services of general interest. i think that is the political stake of this whole debate. the political majorities, i wish they were different, but they are what they are at the moment.
in writing. air pollution is a major public health concern. for example, fine particles emitted by certain human activities are thought to reduce the life expectancy of europeans by about 9 months. in belgium alone, nearly 13 000 people die prematurely each year, mainly from particulate matter emitted by diesel engines. the number of preventable respiratory diseases should prompt legislators to send a simple message: clean air for europe requires drastic pollution standards. that is not the message that the european parliament is sending today by adopting my colleague mr holger krahmer's report on the cafe directive. i regret that on a major citizen issue, the impact of pollution on human health, my colleagues have lacked ambition, aimed above all at not affecting certain industrial sectors. i hope that on second reading the european parliament will correct its tone, be more receptive to the arguments of environmental health specialists and avoid the pitfall of regulations setting less stringent standards than those currently in force. because with this vote, california has never seemed so far away!
in writing. it is a scandal of the highest order that parliament today is significantly weakening the existing air quality regulations. for the first time, parliament is actually taking a weaker position than the council. however, there is ample scientific evidence that fine dust pollution causes some 3.6 million years of life lost. that's 348,000 premature deaths every year. by 2020, air pollution caused by ozone and fine particulate matter will cost europe between eur 189 and 609 billion annually! the ep's position is significantly below the world health organisation's recommendations and those levels have been shown to be achievable in large cities in industrialised countries. the commission is also proposing to extend the deadline for the adoption of the directive to 31 december 2004 by a further two months. octopus politics, you mean. the united states, which already in 1997 issued an annual limit value of 15 μg/m3 for pm2.5, proves that a stricter approach is feasible! that limit must be reached by 2010 at the latest. a state like california goes even further. since 2003, a limit value of 12 μg/m3 has been applied. the ep wants to get it to 20 μg/m3 by ... 2015! if the council does not provide sufficient counterbalance to this weakening, the commission must withdraw the proposal.

president of the council. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, this very good and thorough debate has brought to the fore the challenges and problems associated with the accession negotiations with turkey. but it has also clearly shown the enormous strategic importance of turkey to the european union. as the presidency, we will promote the accession negotiations in accordance with the general principles of enlargement. these general principles are equal treatment of applicants and advancement on the basis of merit. it is worth noting once again that the european council last confirmed in the summer of 2006 that it will respect existing commitments on enlargement and that work will continue on this basis. there are many challenges for turkey, but it is clear that the accession negotiations support the continuation of the reform process in turkey, and that is in everyone's interest. one of the key issues is that turkey must ratify and implement the ankara additional protocol. we are currently working actively to find a solution which would allow direct trade between the european union and northern cyprus and ensure that turkey would also open its ports to cypriot vessels. it is important to find a comprehensive solution to the cyprus problem within the united nations framework, at the same time as the accession negotiations. i thank you, mr president, and members for this very useful and active debate.

european union is ensuring that this is the case. nor does she make herself a tutor who teaches with a stick. let us remember that turkey has put itself forward as a candidate. she took that step. it par écrit. – j'ai décidé de m'abstenir pour le vote définitif du rapport d'initiative de camiel eurlings portant sur les progrès accomplis par la turquie sur la voie de l'adhésion. je conteste en effet l'opportunité et la pertinence d'un tel rapport alors que la commission européenne, chargée de suivre les progrès réalisés par la turquie sur la voie de l'adhésion, n'a pas encore publié le sien. c'est à la commission européenne seule que revient la compétence de juger des progrès réalisés par la turquie. de plus, le rapport eurlings m'a paru déséquilibré. les efforts que ce rapport demande à la turquie sur de nombreux thèmes, dont la liberté d'expression, les droits des minorités et les droits des femmes, sont bien entendu indispensables, mais il reconnait trop peu les avancées de la turquie sur la voie de l'etat de droit et du respect des droits humains. la turquie fait d'importants efforts depuis le dépôt de sa candidature pour respecter les critères d'adhésion; elle a certes toujours de nombreux progrès à faire, mais elle est sur la bonne voie. les amendements votés lors de la session plénière, notamment celui du pse qui supprime la reconnaissance du génocide arménien comme condition préalable à toute adhésion, ont légèrement rééquilibré le rapport eurlings. cependant, cela ne m'a pas totalement satisfait.
the rapporteur. mr president, commissioner, the report we are going to vote on today, i hope, is a direct follow-up to the three commission communications which constitute the so-called 'development aid effectiveness package' and is therefore part of the broader framework of pursuing the objective of improving european development cooperation. that's for context. as regards the challenge, i would even say the challenge, it is very clear: how are we europeans going to significantly improve the effective aid we are giving to the countries of the south? in other words, how are we going to ensure that the aid we give to the countries of the south is translated much more systematically into concrete advances that can really change the lives of millions of people living in conditions that are humanly unacceptable? the collective awareness of what our help could be improved on has taken place. political commitments have been made to make this a priority of our development cooperation policy. the european consensus on development exists and so does the new strategy for africa. that's a great thing. the legislative and technical framework is being put in place. we have to make all this happen, if i may say so. the very definition of development cooperation and therefore what each member state is legitimately entitled to count as official development aid, the minimum level of aid to be reached to meet our commitments, the unbundling of aid which the member states - some of them, in any case - are clearly reluctant to apply, the absence of indicators to assess the progress that will actually be made in improving european aid, or the concern of the actors on the ground that there is a certain backwardness in the involvement of the beneficiary countries in the strategies and programmes for them, are some of the many issues we have dealt with in this report. in addition to these specific questions, which allow us to grasp in part the extent of the work we still have to do to improve our aid in practice, the 3 cs, complementarity of actions, coordination of programmes and coherence of policies, also constitute a framework for work, a particularly important reading of the action we will have to take in the coming years in the field of cooperation. as far as the complementarity of the measures, whether sectoral or geographical, is concerned, the division of labour is bound to give rise to a number of resistances and difficulties. with openness and boldness, we must be able to raise it. the debate cannot be reduced to a confrontation between the protectionism of the member states and the centralism of brussels, even if it is true that it is not unnecessary to warn against excessive centralism consisting in adopting a top-down approach to programming characterised in particular by a reduced participation of the partner countries and civil society in the definition of strategies and priorities. however, centralised coordination between member states and the commission would have undoubted advantages and would, in particular, avoid the situation of a multitude of different actors doing the same thing in the same country or region. the donor atlas, for example, clearly shows that there are forgotten crises, orphan countries as they are called. in addition to post-tsunami situations where the recipient countries are unable to absorb a large amount of one-off aid. although coordination has been discussed for years, the challenges of harmonising procedures and better coordinating the various cooperation programmes of the union remain enormous. moreover, policy coherence concerns the approach we adopt both at the level of the different geographical areas where our development policy is applied and at the level of the various community policies themselves. this is a point on the agenda of the finnish presidency, which has decided to devote a significant part of its work to this subject. it is therefore wise to limit ourselves in our report to stressing its importance until we see what the near future holds, but i would like to take this opportunity to raise this question which seems to me to be essential and which ultimately touches the very foundation of any cooperation initiative, or even any political project. at the moment, as we know, and as we regularly hear, boats continue to reach the spanish coast, our coast, with hundreds of people on board every day trying to escape a fate they have decided to refuse at the risk of their lives. this situation obviously raises questions of migration management, border control or integration policy for immigrant populations. it also raises the question, and this is a very important question, of the effectiveness of our cooperation policy and of its coherence with the other policies we pursue. what is cooperation - without wanting to sink into the caricature - a cooperation of more than 40 years, at the end of which the populations we claim to help aspire to only one thing: to flee their living conditions at all costs? let's not be afraid of words, for me, it could be called failure. what does a cooperation policy granting 50 billion euros a year mean to the countries of the south when it is combined with a policy requiring them to honour a debt whose annual repayment is four times that amount? how can we continue to declare our determination to work for the development of the countries of the south and at the same time continue to impose on them the rules of free trade which they are unable to accept on the terms we impose on them? how can we agree to fight poverty, when at the same time nothing is done to combat the structural causes of poverty? in fact, however effective development cooperation policy may be, it will never be able to meet the many challenges facing the countries of the global south on its own. this is precisely the reason why it needs to be improved, because the more effective european development cooperation is, the more it will have succeeded in making europe aware of the need to pursue a comprehensive policy geared to the pursuit of a common priority objective: the emergence of a more just and united world.
i voted against this joint motion for a resolution because the politically correct discourse on immigration, which is an inviolable medieval dogma in this institution, is also reflected in this resolution. the european parliament has been a very active participant in the process of european integration, and it has been a very active participant in the process of european integration. if we want to assimilate willing immigrants in europe, we must also dare to say that those who cannot make this will must return to their countries of origin. we must then also dare to say that there must be a watertight immigration stop, coupled with an ambitious development policy that reduces the pressure of immigration from the third world. we must then dare to say that there is no place in europe for islamic fundamentalism. the swiss people made a clear choice in a referendum last sunday to preserve their identity. europe would do better to follow the example of the independent and free swiss rather than to bow to the intellectual terror of the immigration lobby.
in writing. the emergence of new sciences always arouses enthusiasm, hope, questioning and even opposition. nanotechnologies: the sciences that deal with objects the size of atoms are no exception! as a liberal member of parliament, i am naturally inclined to support the work of researchers in nanoscience and the control of the assembly of atoms. a revolution that holds great potential for technological applications in objects as diverse as vehicles, food, medicine and regenerative medicine. europe's position in nanotechnology in relation to global competition needs to be strengthened. budgets must be released in addition to the 610 million euros per year of the 7th fp7! it is also essential to provide clear answers to citizens who are concerned about the potential toxicity of nanoparticles to the environment, the food chain and the body. citizenship is not something that is decreed, it is something that is earned and requires education, patience and transparency. the union and the member states must therefore avoid repeating here the mistakes made in the gmo dossier, where opacity in information and action has led to mistrust and rejection of a science that is promising by a large number of european citizens!
madam president, on 20 october, the european heads of state or government will also be discussing immigration in lahti, as i have said. the question is, however, what conclusions they will draw. two weeks ago, ladies and gentlemen, a tragedy struck for the first time with unfortunate asylum seekers off the coast of europe. once again, it was the fault of unscrupulous traffickers who want to bring economic fortune seekers to europe for a lot of money. the european union is also calling for the immediate release of all those who have been detained in the country. most of the people on board were barely saved. but to prevent such tragedies in the future - on lampedusa, in the canary islands and everywhere on our external borders - europe must send clear signals, because gentle healers make stinking wounds. there must be a tough crackdown on the traffickers. they can't be punished enough. secondly, we need a strong asylum policy, one which makes it clear to the rest of the world that europe is serious about protecting its borders. i would therefore like to congratulate the swiss on the wise choice they have made in all their cantons, in choosing to tighten up immigration conditions and the right to asylum, because paradoxically it is the wisest way to pursue a coherent, humane and just policy on these issues. i would therefore like to express the hope that the european heads of state or government will reflect on the swiss example at the informal summit in lahti. europe must learn to listen to its citizens on major social issues. we have seen, for example, how the eurocracy arrogantly wants to continue with a constitution which was rejected by the majority of the population, as in france and the netherlands. the people's voice is often heard in a very different way than when decisions are made over their heads by a caste that is alien to the world. this is no doubt true of the pressing immigration issue.
on behalf of the pse group. mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would first like to thank mrs jeggle for her excellent report. we shall be voting tomorrow on this result of long work and i can only rejoice at the common position we have reached. however, i would like to mention three fundamental points which are also mentioned in the report and which i would like to emphasize. firstly, i would like to stress the need to speed up the development, validation and approval of alternative methods to animal testing. we must give ourselves the financial and logistical means to find different methods of avoiding these trials. we must also limit and strictly regulate these where no alternative is yet possible. secondly, we must not forget that the main consequence of improving animal welfare is an additional cost for european producers. i therefore stress the need to value their efforts through labels and information campaigns to explain the reason for a differentiated price. european producers must not be penalized for complying with the requirements of the european union and improving our food, and consumers must know that price increases are the result of improved food. the introduction of a european label and the organisation of appropriate marketing and information campaigns will enable consumers to identify products which are particularly respectful of animal welfare. thirdly, animal welfare must not be a european value only. the measures we adopt within the framework of the union must also be respected by all the countries from which we import meat. this is an economic and qualitative imperative. the european commission must therefore do its utmost, with the help of international organisations such as the council of europe or the world organisation for animal health, to ensure that the wto pays more attention to animal welfare and harmonises upwards the measures relating to it. the long-term objective must be clearly to achieve the highest and most uniform level of animal protection possible worldwide.

in writing. the liberal and intellectual professions are so called because they differ from other service providers in several respects. the fact that we must guide them so that they can make the greatest possible contribution to the lisbon objectives and respect the fundamental principles of the treaty does not mean that we should ignore their specific nature. the ehler report does acknowledge this specificity; that's why i can support it. the rapporteur, mr ehler, has dealt with his report in the right way: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject of the future of the european union. this is in the interests of the customers (entrepreneurs & consumers/clients), of the mutual competitive and collegial relations within the professional group and of the general interest.
the essential question in this debate is the following: how can the future adapted schengen information system be integrated into a common european strategy to deal with the two greatest challenges facing europe today, namely the mass illegal immigration with its consequences that we see every day and the danger of islamic terrorism? that is the central question in this whole debate. an information system, in which all countries cooperate fully and which is extended in function from the original system, could be of great help in the detection and deportation of illegal immigrants and also in the fight against terrorism. but this information system was already dealt a serious blow last year when the european court of justice ruled that a country cannot refuse access to anyone simply because they are listed in the system as a danger to public order. the court held that a country must be able to determine whether the person concerned constitutes a threat. the deeper and more fundamental question is whether the individual member states themselves accept the logic and the spirit of the schengen system. for example, the regularisation of illegals, people who have entered the schengen area illegally, is a clear violation of the schengen agreement. in short, the information system must be placed within a broader framework. it only makes sense if the member states effectively guard the external borders, have an active and consistent policy of detection and expulsion and take strong action against the smugglers who make a lot of money at the poorly guarded external borders.
member of the commission. mr president, i should like to begin by thanking the rapporteur, mrs isler béguin, for her excellent work and report on this proposal. i would also like to thank mrs gutiérrez for her very positive approach and to assure both the two ladies and all the members of parliament that the commission will work to facilitate the rapprochement and the rapid reaching of an agreement with the council, i should stress that since september 2004 - when the college of commissioners adopted the commission's proposal - about a year after the first reading, we have made a great deal of progress with life+ and the european commission's position has changed significantly on a number of issues compared to its first proposal. i believe that the council's common position, with which the commission largely agrees, meets many of parliament's basic demands. the draft regulation now includes a section on nature and biodiversity. life+ may finance measures and activities for the exchange of views and best practices or demonstration projects, including those relating to the management and designation of natura 2000 sites and to habitats and bird guidelines. the conservation aspect of life+ is important. at least 40% of the resources will be allocated to expenditure in this area. i would like to stress that this is the minimum rate and that member states can spend a higher rate if they wish. as regards the financing of natura 2000, the commission has secured funding opportunities through the structural funds, the fisheries fund and the rural development programme under the common agricultural policy. the commission may, of course, encourage natura 2000 spending, but in accordance with the principle of subsidiarity, member states have the competence and discretion to decide to what extent they wish to make use of these funding possibilities. the commission, for its part, will do its utmost to ensure that member states take advantage of funding opportunities wherever possible. the opinion of the directorate-general for the environment is always sought before the adoption of strategic reference frameworks and operational programmes for the structural funds, as well as rural development programmes submitted by member states. i repeat that one of our first priorities is to ensure that natura 2000 is adequately funded and i therefore very much appreciate parliament's support in this regard. however, the most important issue for discussion tonight is the method of implementation of the programme. the common position provides for 80% of the funding for life+ to be allocated to the member states. i regard parliament's preference for maintaining central management of the programme as a sign of confidence in the commission. the commission's proposal is to be submitted to the council for adoption. the method proposed in the common position is therefore preferable. first, it is in line with the principle of subsidiarity: it provides the greatest possible flexibility to meet the different needs of the member states. secondly, it ensures that all countries receive a minimum share of funding. under the current life 3 system, they often don't get paid. thirdly, it is transparent and auditable and ensures that the added value for the european union is maintained. as you know, the transfer of management responsibilities is at the heart of the commission's proposal. the member states are building on this and are already preparing draft programmes. the commission cannot therefore accept the various amendments concerning the deletion of all references to the transfer of management responsibilities. unfortunately, the outcome of the informal meetings between parliament, the council and the commission following the vote in the committee on the environment on 14 september did not allow us to make any significant progress towards reaching an agreement on the transfer of management responsibilities. however, despite the differences, we all support the life+ programme and its implementation as soon as possible so that resources can be released and there is no delay in the implementation of the programmes. member states have over €2 billion for the life+ programme. it is necessary to find a way of disbursing this money as quickly as possible, even if it means compromises on all sides. we must therefore continue to work together to find practical solutions, particularly as regards the method of implementing life+. in this context, we will make every effort to reach a compromise between the council and parliament and will mediate to facilitate an agreement.

the rapporteur. mr president, thank you for being here. the european union-syria association agreement is the missing piece in the barcelona process. but it is also the european parliament's arles affair: so many times announced and never concluded. negotiations on this agreement began in 1996, even though syria has not withdrawn from lebanon. they are difficult but they lead to a project that has been revised many times and which includes the non-proliferation of weapons of mass destruction and the fight against terrorism. the agreement was initialled in october 2004 in the council, but the ratification process was interrupted by the heinous assassination of former prime minister hariri on 14 february 2005. we know what happens next. voices in lebanon and around the world immediately point the finger at the regime in damascus. an investigation, first by judge mehlis and now by judge brammetz, is being carried out on the spot, while resolution 1559 calls for the withdrawal of syrian troops and intelligence services from lebanon. the presumption of innocence is a pillar of our legal systems, but the precautionary principle must not be forgotten. while syria has been diligent and has quickly withdrawn its troops from lebanon, it was initially reluctant to cooperate with judge mehlis. europe has therefore cautiously distanced itself from the regime in damascus. is this caution still appropriate today, when various developments, both on the international scene and in the syrian domestic political system, should prompt us to reopen dialogue? the council will decide, but i am in favour of dialogue and the committee on foreign affairs has given me a lot of support in this direction. this dialogue could eventually lead to the signing of the agreement, but this is a premature issue at the moment and is not the subject of the recommendation. what are these events? first, the resumption of effective cooperation with the investigation of judge brammetz, which is attested to in his last report. secondly, the fact that by isolating syria, we have been counterproductive to the internal democracy of the country, without weakening a strong regime. our the european parliament has a duty to protect the environment. mr president, let me first of all thank mrs schroedter for the long and excellent work she has done on this report, and in particular for her search for the widest possible consensus on a text which is important in more than one way. it also recalled the useful work carried out by a large majority of members of this parliament in the debate and vote on the draft directive on services. this is useful because by voting in favour of fundamental amendments which ensure absolute respect for the competences and subjects covered by the posting of workers directive, parliament guaranteed the essential role that the directive must play at a time when we are experiencing and will experience a sharp increase in the provision of services and an increase in the mobility of workers in the eu-25. the text also guaranteed - and i believe that was the unanimous view of the members of the committee on employment and social affairs - our competence in matters which are our responsibility by law. a directive that is well understood, well applied and whose effects are well controlled must strike the right balance between fair competition and guarantees of respect for workers' rights. the rules of the game must be strict and fair. they must also ensure transparency and security for workers, both in the provision of services and in the freedom of movement. a well-understood directive requires a major effort of explanation, administrative simplification and information. a well-implemented directive must clearly enforce the right to free movement and the principles of equal treatment and pursue the objectives of harmonizing working conditions and social systems. a directive with well-controlled effects is a major tool in the fight against anti-european sentiments that can be generated in the population by the presence of workers treated as mere commodities displaced at leisure and exploited under the lowest conditions.

in writing. directive 96/71/ec on the posting of workers is an important milestone in european labour law. the schroedter report highlights the problems of the application of the directive and thus responds to a commission communication, which was promised in 2004 but only published in april 2006. in this context, the commission is seeking to limit the control measures available to the member states by relying on judgments of the court of justice. the schroedter report redraws the balance between economic liberalisation and social protection. for example, certain administrative measures (e.g. social inspection of the situation of the selfemployed, collective agreements) can no longer be regarded as restricting the free movement of workers on the labour markets of the european union, as the court of justice has also ruled. the minimum wage, working conditions and health and safety at work and the host country principle are important instruments in preventing social dumping and unfair competition. they also ensure fair treatment of employees and service providers. the neoliberal course which europe has been following for years now is being given a more social twist by this report. the european parliament must take up its responsibility for the social protection of workers and that is why i support the schroedter report.

in writing. in january 2005, the european parliament approved my report on the european environment and health action plan 2004-2010 by a very large majority. among the recommendations made was the phasing out of certain chemicals which, like mercury used in dental amalgams and in non-electronic monitoring devices, are of high concern to human health. and they need to be replaced by safer alternatives. the 33 tonnes of mercury used annually in the european union are, like other heavy metals, a cause of neurological diseases, particularly affecting children. a public health imperative must now prevail in our chemically pervasive societies! i therefore regret that 327 of my colleagues, contrary to the opinion of our rapporteur, mrs sornosa, have decided to limit the prohibitions on the placing on the market of articles (thermometers, barometers) containing mercury and intended for the general public. tomorrow, however, we will have to go further and require the 25 member states to take ambitious measures to remove mercury in all its forms from sensitive public places such as maternity wards, day nurseries, hospitals and schools.

in writing. the council's common position on the services directive as it is put to the vote today is an improvement on the commission's original proposal, but some points remain unacceptable. the text creates legal uncertainty. the legislation applicable and the extent to which member states may impose national provisions are not clearly defined. the possibility of upward harmonisation is not included. the revision clause also opens the door to reintroducing the principle of the country of origin and to adding services to the scope of the directive which are now excluded. services of general economic interest are covered by the directive and, in the absence of a framework directive on services of general interest, this directive threatens to constitute a horizontal framework for services of general economic interest. i also regret that the initially indicative list of social services excluded from the directive has been converted into a restrictive list. the absence of any reference to the charter of fundamental rights is also disappointing. finally, i am particularly displeased at the capitulation of the ep in pursuing to the full the achievements of the first reading. this undermines the future of a democratic and social europe.

president of the council. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, in recent years the questions of weapons of mass destruction and arms control have become central to the european union's common foreign and security policy. however, multilateral arms control and disarmament activities have faced major challenges and achieved limited results in recent years. the need for a change in this trend is very great. the third review conference of the convention on conventional arms is currently under way in geneva. this conference, together with the sixth review conference of the biological weapons convention (bwc) to be held in mid-november, will both provide an opportunity to demonstrate that the international community is capable of responding effectively to today's arms control and disarmament challenges. the civilian population is all too often the victim of weapons covered by the convention on the prohibition of conventional weapons, known as the ccw. the burden of clearing unexploded war remains an ever-increasing burden on the international community. the universalisation of the fifth protocol on non-explosive remnants of war and its effective implementation require the work of all of us. the european union welcomed the entry into force of the protocol during the review conference. the protocol on unexploded ordnance of war was drawn up in response to the humanitarian threat posed to civilians by unexploded conventional munitions, including cargo munitions, after the end of war. recent events in southern lebanon are a reminder of the dangers posed to civilians by unexploded war debris, in particular by mortars, and the difficulty and cost of removing it. we must act immediately. the eu has therefore proposed that the expert level work on the ccw be continued in 2007. the current mandate includes the development of the technical characteristics of different types of ammunition in a preventive manner, in order to reduce the humanitarian risk they pose. in addition, several member states of the european union have joined the proposal at national level to start negotiations on a legally binding protocol to respond to the humanitarian dangers posed by live ammunition. however, the member states of the european union do not share a common position on the specific treatment limited to cargo ammunition under the ccw. this morning, the review conference agreed to strengthen the union's position on live ammunition and decided to propose the setting up of an expert group. it is intended to prepare recommendations on further measures to deal with loaded ammunition in the context of the convention on conventional weapons. in the five years of negotiations on anti-vehicle mines, the eu has consistently stressed that our aim is a legally binding protocol that reinforces and adds value to international humanitarian law. it seems unlikely that the minutes will be agreed at the review conference. for more than 30 years, the biological weapons convention has served as the normative and legal cornerstone of biological disarmament and non-proliferation. biosciences are one of the fastest-growing fields of science today, with unlimited potential for both good and evil. the ban must be able to adapt to scientific developments and meet the challenges it poses. the review conferences provide an opportunity for this. the european union has made comprehensive preparations for the sixth review conference of the btwc in line with the objectives of the eu strategy on mass destruction. these objectives include, in particular, the universalisation of the convention, the enhancement of national implementation and compliance with the convention and the strengthening of the convention. the council adopted a common position on the sixth review conference in february this year. the union's position balances a realistic and ambitious approach, but calls for a comprehensive review of the agreement and for practical improvements in its implementation. in addition to the common position, the union has prepared thematic working papers on a number of subjects. in addition to joint actions, the european union has agreed on an action plan for the biological weapons convention. in this context, the member states committed themselves to taking action to strengthen the agreement, such as the annual notification of confidence-building measures. these concrete actions will add credibility to the key objectives set out in the eu common position on the review conference on the btwc. the conditions for a review conference on the convention on conventional arms control are good, though perhaps not optimal. we are, however, optimistic about the success of the conference. the sixth review conference on the prohibition of biological weapons also has every chance of a successful outcome.
vice-president of the commission. mr president, weapons of mass destruction are potentially the greatest threat to european security and, in terms of the ability to inflict mass casualties, the weapons of most concern are clearly nuclear and biological. while we all carry with us the collective memory of the horrors of hiroshima and nagasaki, there are no equivalent common images for biological weapons. mass death from biological agents, or diseases like the plague, appear by contrast to be part of ‘ancient history’ or science fiction. we must hope that it stays that way, but we cannot be complacent. consider the following. firstly, unlike nuclear weapons, biological weapons are not dependent on limited supplies of weapons-grade uranium and plutonium. the rapid development and dissemination of biosciences is bringing weapons capabilities within reach of those who could do us harm. the danger that humans, animals or plants might be attacked by biological agents is a real one. secondly, official reports suggest that terrorists are seeking bio-weapons. if terrorists were to start biological attacks across europe, many lives would be at risk and our lifestyle could change dramatically. thirdly, biological weapons have been used in the recent past. the anthrax attacks in the united states in late 2001 killed few people but caused significant, wider social and political disruption, and the perpetrators remain at large. how protected are we today? all use and possession of biological weapons has been prohibited since the biological weapons convention came into force in 1975. however, this convention is the weakest of the regimes addressing weapons of mass destruction. it has no provision for verification of compliance or implementation, and, unlike the nuclear non-proliferation treaty and the chemical weapons convention, there is no central institution with oversight. furthermore, there are fewer parties to the convention than to either of those two treaties. our security would be enhanced, therefore, if the effectiveness of the biological and toxic weapons convention could be strengthened and its implementation improved. state parties should be encouraged to address these weaknesses at the forthcoming review conference later this month. good security starts at home. my colleague, commissioner frattini, is well aware of the need for action. the commission is examining potential responses through a common framework approach. national responses to biological threats are clearly insufficient. alongside the work on strengthening public health and medical surveillance and response, we will be issuing a green paper on european bio-preparedness in spring 2007. disaster and disease do not respect borders. the issue of bio-threats is one where internal and external policies are inextricably linked. much can be achieved through better international collaboration and coordination. we will aim to share best practice on bio-preparedness: by helping to protect others, we will better protect ourselves. i would also like to say a few words on a very different type of deadly weapon. unexploded cluster ammunition represents a threat to human security and sustainable development. such munitions kill innocent people every day and 30% of those killed are children. they represent major challenges, both politically and to daily life in former war zones. a review conference is currently under way. it will see protocol v to the convention, on ‘explosive remnants of war’ – i.e. failed explosive ordnance, ammunition or sub-munitions from cluster bombs, – enter into force. the implementation of this protocol, intended to eradicate the threat that such legacies of war pose to populations, will be a welcome step forward. the events in lebanon this summer highlighted the issue, particularly in view of the high failure rate of the cluster sub-munitions employed. helping lebanon in the difficult task of clearing these deadly remnants of war is important and we have contributed some eur 5 million to the task. we also support the need to focus more attention on strengthening and ensuring compliance with international humanitarian law in this area. commission assistance and cooperation in humanitarian disarmament – that is to say mine action, clearing the explosive remnants of war and fighting the illicit spread of small arms – all have a positive impact on improving human security. they contribute to the sustainability of our development policies. we are grateful for the support of the european parliament in president, the production and use of biological weapons used to be the prerogative of states. today, advances in biotechnology mean that fewer and fewer resources are needed to produce dangerous viruses, bacteria and poisons. so terrorist organizations could also resort to biological warfare. interpol noted in march of this year that there were serious indications that al-qaeda was preparing to commit acts of biological terrorism. the devastation that could be wrought in this way is far greater than that of the attacks in new york, madrid and london. you can't make treaties with terrorists the way states make treaties with each other. it is of great importance that the police services continue to be trained in preventing and combating bioterrorism. legislation may need to be adjusted here and there to control sensitive scientific research. the exchange of information between intelligence services within the european union, but also with our partners in the united states, russia and all other nations willing to take responsibility in the fight against terrorism, remains a focus.
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, i wish to begin by warmly congratulating mr von wogau on his excellent report, which offers an accurate and comprehensive survey of the current line of thinking of the committee on foreign affairs and the subcommittee on security and defence. i also want to thank him for the efforts he made to reach the broadest possible agreement with the other political groups. while this clearly means that my group will approve the report, i would like to stress two particular points. the first point is the need to ensure effective parliamentary control, both at national and eu levels. the eu, as has been said, is currently engaged in a growing number of military and civilian operations in various parts of the world. i insist on the need for parliament to be continually updated and to be informed and consulted whenever a new operation is being contemplated. until now, all these operations have gone well, but it is entirely possible that one day a major incident will occur and there will then be widespread clamour for parliamentary oversight. secondly, much greater clarification of the respective roles and responsibilities of the commission, the high representative, the council and the member states is also needed. who pays what is too often decided on a case-by-case basis, which again makes oversight difficult. this is conducive to overlap and waste and, worst of all, also leads to outright competition between, for instance, the council’s special envoys and the commission’s delegations. both the council and the commission will of course deny this is the case, but a number of us know better, even if, luckily, such competition is not the general rule. finally, what is needed most of all is political will on the part of member state governments and that, alas, is an entirely different story.

mr president, a white paper on european communication policy is a good idea. what a thought to have waited so long! this is perhaps the only positive effect of the rejection of the constitution in france and the netherlands. it will have put europe's leaders face to face with their enormous responsibilities in terms of communication. europe does not suffer from a democratic deficit, the trial is unfair, but from a deficit of information, explanation, adjusted communication, interactive and understandable. i am glad that this white paper has been produced, but i am sorry that it is limited to questions and principles. there is no longer any need to talk about forums, consultations, surveys, networks and wondering what action to take - it must be taken. the three main points of this document are, in my view, points 23, 24 and 32. it is in schools that the battle for european citizenship is won. we experience it every day in our meetings with the students. it is in higher education that, thanks to erasmus, the true citizens of europe are cultivated, who are familiar with our cultures and our differences, and it is with the traditional media, i do not believe in the alternative media, that we must work to highlight the daily value of our activities and to decode the added value of our legislation. i conclude by saying, mr president, that this challenge is immense and that this debate is essential, because the real threat in europe today is not scepticism but indifference. and our weapon against it is communication.

member of the commission. mr president, the commission shares parliament’s concerns and welcomes the opportunity to discuss bangladesh today. the country is at a critical juncture of its fragile democracy. much is at stake with the forthcoming elections in this densely populated country. regrettably, however, the institutional framework and the acrimonious relations between the two main political parties do not bode well for a peaceful and democratic electoral process. over the last weeks and months, the commission and the seven member states with representations in dhaka, as well as
